## Security efficacy/ROI infographics — honestly showing 'AI/automation reduced analyst workload by X%' (hero numbers, reduction ratios, anti-overclaiming) for the TLSOC dashboard


**Key findings:**
- The dominant vendor pattern is a FUNNEL narrative, not a single percentage: raw alerts -> correlated/enriched groups -> risk-ranked cases -> auto-resolved vs escalated vs analyst-confirmed. Elastic: '1,000+ alerts/day -> 8 actionable discoveries' + '75% average FP reduction'. eSentire: '10,000 alerts -> 10 stories', '2,000-4,000 events/day -> 8-15 attack chains'. secure.com: '174 alerts/day = 78% noise (136) vs 22% signal (38)', '65% filtered automatically / 35% reaches analyst'. Every credible version names WHO/WHAT filtered each stage (correlation engine vs policy vs analyst), not an unattributed black box. (Elastic blog; eSentire blog; secure.com blog)
- Vendors increasingly pair a 'hero' reduction number with a companion QUALITY metric so speed can't substitute for correctness. UnderDefense's AI-SOC SLA framework requires False-Positive-Reduction AND AI Decision Accuracy (>95%) AND Alert Coverage Rate (100% investigated, vs the 50-70% industry norm) AND Escalation Rate kept in a defined 5-15% 'optimal band' (too low = suppression risk, too high = no automation benefit) reported together, monthly, with mean/median/P95 all disclosed because 'averaging masks outliers.' (underdefense.com/blog/ai-soc-sla-guide)
- Gartner's published 7-question framework for evaluating AI SOC agents (Lawson & Davies, cited via BleepingComputer/others) puts outcome measurement beyond raw alert count (MTTR/MTTC, not alert volume), explainability/audit trail, and autonomy boundaries/fail-safes at the center — 'many AI vendors claim to reduce alerts but fail to demonstrate a reduction in MTTR.' Transparency (how the agent explains its own decisions) is called out as possibly the single most important criterion. (bleepingcomputer.com, gartner.com/en/documents/7121630)
- A March 2026 Help Net Security analysis (citing analyst Anton Chuvakin and Rochford) names the specific overclaiming failure modes to avoid: (1) the 'prophecy pattern' — describing a future capability ('autonomous investigations', 'analyst replacement') as if it's a present, repeatable one; (2) unfalsifiable claims like '50% faster investigations' with no stated baseline/methodology; (3) blaming low adoption on 'user change-resistance' rather than product immaturity — a red flag of vendor self-unawareness; (4) counting feature-activation/'exploring' as 'adoption'. Recommended honest pattern instead: narrow, specific, reproducible claims ('Mean-time-to-Verdict reduced to 7 minutes for account-hijack triage in AWS'), explicit acknowledgment of where AI still needs a human, multi-state (not binary) outcomes, mandatory circuit-breakers when confidence drops, and post-incident explainability so an analyst can re-derive the verdict. (helpnetsecurity.com/2026/03/26/future-ai-soc-vendor-claims)
- Torq's own 'AI SOC metrics that matter' guidance explicitly warns against vanity metrics that are common in vendor decks: 'alerts processed' (volume without outcome — 'processing 10,000 alerts means nothing if 9,500 didn't need investigation'), 'time saved per alert' (ignores whether the alert warranted investigation at all), and 'AI accuracy' stated with no denominator/context (can hide failure on hard cases). Recommends establishing a pre-deployment BASELINE per org and comparing against it, rather than trusting a vendor's cross-org number. (torq.io/blog/ai-soc-metrics)
- SANS 2025 Detection & Response survey context (useful as an honest 'industry baseline' comparator, not a self-congratulatory hero number): 73% of orgs name false positives their #1 detection challenge (up sharply YoY); ~46% of all alerts are false positives; Trend Micro found analysts spend >25% of time on FPs and 51% feel overwhelmed by volume. This is the credible 'before' state any efficacy claim should be measured against. (stamus-networks.com, torq.io/blog/sans-2025-soc-survey)
- IBM Cost of a Data Breach 2025 shows the credible large-N form of an ROI hero stat done right: it's a MEASURED, denominator-disclosed, third-party study, not a vendor's own case study — 'orgs using AI/automation extensively: $3.62M avg breach cost vs $5.52M without' and 'detection+containment cycle cut by ~80 days' (241 days mean overall, lowest in 9 years). The credibility comes from large sample, independent methodology, and a stated cost delta, not just a percentage. (ibm.com/reports/data-breach, ibm.com/think/x-force/2025-cost-of-a-data-breach-navigating-ai)
- Even vendor marketing copy self-polices with disclaimer language when numbers could be read as guarantees, e.g. Elastic's blog frames its case-study figure explicitly as 'one organisation' and separately disclaims forward-looking-feature language ('release and timing... remain at Elastic's sole discretion... may not be delivered on time or at all') — i.e. named-N attribution and no implied universality are treated as due diligence, not just legal boilerplate. (elastic.co/blog/reduce-alert-fatigue-with-ai-defence-soc)
- Realistic, credible 'time reclaimed' vendor numbers are anecdote-scale and attributed, not aggregate averages: Torq customers — Valvoline '6-7 analyst hours/day', Carvana '100% of Tier-1 alert handling automated within one month', HWG Sababa '95% MTTI/MTTR improvement on medium/low-priority cases' (explicitly scoped to a severity tier, not 'all cases'). The scoping to a specific tier/workflow, not a blanket 'AI cut workload by X%', is what keeps these from reading as inflated. (torq.io/blog/ai-soc-automation-results, torq.io/blog/enterprise-automation-bloomreach-case-study)
- Prophet Security's SOC-metrics guidance flags a subtle honesty trap directly relevant to a triage product: 'tuning alerts may artificially raise your mean time to investigate' — i.e., aggressive noise suppression can make remaining metrics look BETTER for the wrong reason (survivorship bias in the surviving alert pool), so a dashboard must track alert-source/tuning-change events alongside MTTI/MTTR trends, not present the trend in isolation. It also recommends showing median alongside mean specifically to resist outlier distortion, and computing realistic 'available analyst hours' (proposes 5.6 of 8 hours/day after breaks/meetings) before claiming capacity freed up. (prophetsecurity.ai/blog/soc-metrics-that-matter)

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Build a `TriageFunnelChart` in `webui/src/soc/components/charts.tsx` (a labeled step/waterfall funnel per the dataviz skill's funnel form) wired live to `engine/metrics.py` counts — NOT a static marketing graphic. Stages should mirror our own pipeline and be honestly attributed to WHO decided: Ingested (connectors) -> Correlated into clusters (`engine/correlation.py`) -> Cases opened -> Auto-closed FALSE_POSITIVE (`decide()`/AutoClosePolicy, code) -> Escalated/needs-human (`decide()`, code) -> Analyst-confirmed (human, `Case.feedback`). Each stage bar shows an absolute count AND is clickable, deep-linking to `/cases?status=...` filtered by that stage so every number is auditable in one click — directly answers Gartner's #1 evaluation criterion (explainability).
- Never render a bare 'AI reduced workload by X%' hero tile. Because `decide()` is deterministic code, not the LLM (non-negotiable #3), the copy must say what actually happened: split into two tiles — 'Automation resolved N of M cases without analyst review' (attributable to policy/AutoClosePolicy, factual) and 'AI investigator recommended escalation on N cases; analysts agreed on X%' (attributable to the LLM verdict, paired with the disagreement count, not hidden). This keeps the UI's own claims as truthful as the backend's non-negotiables already require.
- Add a `sampleSize`/`asOf` secondary line to `KpiTile`/`StatCard` (`webui/src/soc/components/`) — small muted text under every efficacy number reading e.g. 'n=482 cases · last 30d' or '340 of 1,842 cases graded (18% coverage)'. This directly implements the UnderDefense/Gartner 'denominator disclosure' pattern and the Help Net Security 'unfalsifiable claim' critique — no percentage renders without its base count next to it.
- Build the 'AI-analyst agreement rate' tile from the EXISTING `Case.feedback` / `GET /api/feedback/stats` data (already shipped) rather than inventing a number: numerator = cases where analyst grade matches AI verdict, denominator = ALL graded cases in the window, with the graded-vs-total coverage shown as a secondary stat. This is our own equivalent of UnderDefense's 'AI Decision Accuracy' + 'human override frequency' SLA metrics — but computed from real audit data instead of asserted.
- Show MTTA/MTTR/dwell as median AND p90 side-by-side on the same tile (data already computed by `engine/metrics.py`), never a single mean — per the UnderDefense clause-table pattern ('demand clarity on mean vs median vs P95... averaging masks outliers') and Prophet Security's explicit median-over-mean guidance. Bind the existing `TimeRangePicker` component to these tiles so every number recomputes over the selected window instead of being a frozen marketing screenshot.
- Add an 'escalation rate' tile with a visible healthy-band annotation (e.g. a shaded 5-15% zone on a small range/gauge using `RiskGauge`-style visuals) rather than just a naked number — too-low reads as over-suppression risk, too-high reads as automation not pulling weight, directly encoding UnderDefense's 'optimal escalation rate' guidance instead of a context-free percentage.
- Introduce 3 visually distinct `DecisionBadge` variants in `webui/src/soc/components/badges.tsx` — 'Policy auto-closed' (deterministic code, e.g. slate/neutral), 'AI recommends — pending human' (verdict/status axis token, e.g. amber), 'Analyst confirmed' (green) — reused on case rows, the CaseDetail trace timeline, and the funnel chart's stage labels, so a glance at any list tells you whether a number represents code, an unconfirmed model output, or a human-verified fact. This operationalizes the #3 non-negotiable in the UI's visual language, not just in backend logic.
- For any 'hours/cost reclaimed' style tile, do NOT fabricate a rate. Add an explicit, operator-set `Preferences.metrics.avg_manual_triage_minutes` field (Settings, default unset) and only render the tile once it's configured, with copy like 'Based on your configured 12 min/case estimate' next to the number and a Settings deep-link if unset ('Set your average manual triage time to see hours reclaimed'). This matches Prophet Security's 'realistic available analyst hours' guidance and avoids the FTC-flagged 'AI washing' failure mode of asserting a workload-saved number nobody can verify.
- Track and surface alert-source/threshold-tuning change events (already exists via `engine/threshold_tuner.py` audit trail, `ActionType.TUNING`) on the SAME chart as MTTI/FP-rate trend lines (small vertical markers on the trend chart) — directly implements Prophet Security's warning that 'tuning alerts may artificially raise your mean time to investigate' by making tuning events visible context for any trend improvement, instead of letting a rate look like a pure automation win.
- On the Case Detail 'Why' tab (already built, consumes `GET /api/cases/{id}/rationale`), make sure it is the deep-link target from EVERY dashboard hero tile and funnel stage — closing the loop from aggregate claim to individual, inspectable evidence (persona/playbook/memory/knowledge/tools/`decision_rationale`) is exactly the 'post-incident explainability' behavior Help Net Security's sources call the credibility bar for AI SOC vendors.
- In copy/microcopy throughout Overview and Metrics pages, follow the 'capability amplification, not headcount reduction' framing recommended by Chuvakin/Rochford: prefer 'reclaimed analyst capacity for higher-value work' / 'cases resolved without manual touch' over 'replaces analysts' or 'reduces headcount needs' — this is a pure copy change with no engineering cost but avoids the single most-cited overclaiming failure mode in the research.
- Use an industry-baseline comparison card (SANS 2025: '46% of alerts industry-wide are false positives; 73% of teams cite FPs as their #1 challenge') ONLY as read-only context copy in onboarding/wizard or an info tooltip on the funnel chart — never as a claimed 'vs industry' improvement percentage for THIS tenant, since we have no controlled study; keep it clearly labeled as an external citation, not our own measured delta.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Presenting a percentage reduction with no denominator/absolute count ('99% noise reduction') — Torq and UnderDefense both flag this as the #1 vanity-metric pattern; always pair % with N.
- Using 'alerts processed' or 'time saved per alert' as a hero metric — it rewards volume, not correct outcomes (Torq: 'processing 10,000 alerts means nothing if 9,500 didn't need investigation').
- Reporting only the mean when medians/P90 tell a very different (and more honest) story — outliers routinely mask true typical-case performance (Prophet Security, UnderDefense).
- Claiming a speed/reduction number without disclosing whether it came from suppression (dropped/ignored signals) vs. genuine correlation — an aggressive filter can produce a great-looking reduction number while quietly increasing false negatives; always pair reduction metrics with a coverage/escalation-rate or false-negative sanity check.
- Framing automation as 'AI decided X' when in our architecture a deterministic policy (`decide()`) made the close/escalate call — conflating LLM verdict with the actual code-enforced action misleads users about what the AI is actually allowed to do (breaks our own non-negotiable #3 in spirit even if the backend enforces it correctly).
- The 'prophecy pattern': describing a not-yet-general capability ('autonomous investigation', 'replaces Tier-1 analysts') in present tense as if it's already true in production for this tenant — flagged explicitly by Gartner/Help Net Security analysts as the dominant 2026 AI-SOC marketing failure.
- Unfalsifiable comparative claims ('50% faster investigations') with no stated baseline, window, or measurement method — if a number can't be recomputed/audited by the customer, don't ship it as a hero tile.
- Blaming low usage/adoption on 'analyst resistance to change' instead of examining product/UX maturity — a credibility red flag callable out by Chuvakin; don't let our own copy imply this if analysts underuse a feature.
- Static, one-time-computed 'hero' screenshots baked into onboarding/marketing pages that never refresh — unlike vendor case studies we actually have live data, so every efficacy number in-product should be time-windowed and recomputed, not a frozen constant.
- Auto-close-rate tiles that silently include TRUE_POSITIVE auto-closes when that policy is off by default — a dashboard that shows a big 'automation resolved X% of cases' number must respect that non-negotiable #3 keeps NEEDS_HUMAN cases and (by default) TP cases out of any auto-resolved count; mixing them in would overstate autonomous coverage.
- Showing 'AI accuracy: 98%' as a single global number cherry-picked from a small or favorable subset — always disclose the graded-sample coverage (how many of all cases actually got human feedback) alongside the agreement rate, per UnderDefense's 'human override frequency, monthly' requirement.

**Sources:**
- https://www.elastic.co/blog/reduce-alert-fatigue-with-ai-defence-soc
- https://www.esentire.com/blog/from-10-000-alerts-to-10-stories-how-correlated-attack-chains-can-help-beat-soc-burnout
- https://www.secure.com/blog/soc/alerts-to-cases-noise-reduction
- https://torq.io/blog/ai-soc-metrics/
- https://torq.io/blog/ai-soc-automation-results/
- https://torq.io/blog/enterprise-automation-bloomreach-case-study/
- https://torq.io/blog/sans-2025-soc-survey/
- https://www.prophetsecurity.ai/blog/soc-metrics-that-matter-mttr-mtti-false-negatives-and-more
- https://underdefense.com/blog/ai-soc-sla-guide/
- https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/03/26/future-ai-soc-vendor-claims/
- https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/how-to-evaluate-ai-soc-agents-7-questions-gartner-says-you-should-be-asking/

- https://www.stamus-networks.com/blog/what-the-2025-sans-detection-response-survey-reveals-false-positives-alert-fatigue-are-worsening

## Case-detail as narrative: timeline/step UX patterns for presenting a SOC investigation (what fired -> correlation -> assessment -> decision), applied to our StageTimeline/TraceTimeline/WhyPanel in webui/src/soc/pages/CaseDetail.tsx


**Key findings:**
- Microsoft Sentinel's incident page treats the timeline as the literal spine of the story ('Reconstruct the timeline of the attack story'): a single 'Incident timeline' widget mixes alerts + hunting bookmarks in strict chronological order, each item expandable to full detail, sitting next to (not buried under a separate tab from) an Entities widget and a 'Similar incidents' context widget and 'Top insights' (assessment) widget -- facts, entities, and assessment are co-located on ONE Overview screen, with a separate power-user 'Investigation graph' for free-form pivoting. Source: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/sentinel/investigate-incidents
- PagerDuty's Narrative Builder formalizes narrative construction around typed event markers (Detection / Diagnosis / Repair / Key Moment), each with a plain-language Summary ('as if you are telling the story of what happened to a coworker') plus a separate Notes scratch space, built from raw evidence dragged in -- i.e. it explicitly separates the authored narrative sentence from the raw evidence backing it. Source: https://support.pagerduty.com/main/docs/narrative-builder
- Segel & Heer's 'Narrative Visualization: Telling Stories with Data' (IEEE TVCG 2010) identifies the 'Martini Glass' narrative structure -- author-driven strict linear order first (the stem), then free-form reader-directed exploration (the bowl) -- as the canonical genre for this kind of story; it also stresses 'visual narrative tactics' (distinct highlighting/annotation layers for the story's key moment, not just color) and 'narrative structure tactics' (progression ordering, checkpoints). Source: https://medium.com/@gstarnes_58006/research-paper-summarization-for-narrative-visualization-telling-stories-with-data-by-edward-6270b2aff5c3 ; PDF: http://vis.stanford.edu/files/2010-Narrative-InfoVis.pdf
- Agentic-SOC competitors (Dropzone AI, Prophet Security, Radiant Security) converge on the same report shape we should target: BLUF verdict first, then a ranked/ordered list of findings each stating specific evidence, ending in a plain-English conclusion + recommended disposition, with a SEPARATE 'full audit trail' (every question asked, every tool queried) kept available but not the primary read. Dropzone explicitly brands this 'No Black Boxes.' Sources: https://www.dropzone.ai/ai-soc-analyst ; https://www.prophetsecurity.ai/ ; (Radiant via search) https://underdefense.com/blog/dropzone-ai-against-11-rivals-ai-soc-showdown/
- BLUF (Bottom-Line-Up-Front), the US military/intelligence-writing standard, front-loads the conclusion (1-2 sentences) before any supporting evidence, because the reader (an analyst under alert-fatigue-level time pressure) needs the verdict before deciding whether to read further. Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BLUF_(communication) ; https://www.specialeurasia.com/2023/05/11/report-writing-bluf-intelligence/
- Google SecOps (Chronicle) keeps a single 'Case Wall' as the chronological log of ALL case-related activity (creation to closure: alerts, comments, tasks, manual + system actions) -- it does not fragment human collaboration from system/agent activity onto separate pages. Source: https://docs.cloud.google.com/chronicle/docs/soar/investigate/working-with-cases/explore-entities-and-alerts-investigation
- Alert-fatigue research is explicit that context-switching between separate consoles/tabs to reconstruct one incident is itself a cognitive-load driver, and that pre-enriched, already-correlated context lets an analyst assess in seconds instead of the first 10-15 minutes of manual reconstruction. This is a direct argument against spreading one case's story across many tabs. Source: https://panther.com/blog/what-is-alert-fatigue ; https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-032-19540-1_2
- Progressive disclosure (Nielsen, 1995; NN/g) -- defer secondary detail behind an explicit expand action, keep only the decision-relevant summary visible by default -- is the correct model for per-stage/per-span detail; the 'stepper' pattern (one step highlighted/expanded, siblings collapsed) is the standard implementation. Source: https://www.nngroup.com/videos/progressive-disclosure/ ; https://www.uxpin.com/studio/blog/what-is-progressive-disclosure/
- In OUR codebase, the story is already split across THREE separate artifacts that each independently re-tell parts of the same investigation: StageTimeline.tsx (6-stage narrative: input->correlate->risk->triage->investigate->decide, with per-stage collapsible ReAct-shaped steps and a `deterministic` badge), TraceTimeline.tsx (the raw ReAct span log with a visually distinct bordered `DecisionStep` card for the terminal decide() call), and WhyPanel.tsx (decision/reasoning/knowledge/tools re-rendered again as separate cards) -- these live on 3 of CaseDetail.tsx's 8 tabs (`timeline`, `trace`, `why`), which is exactly the tab-fragmentation alert-fatigue research warns against.
- In StageTimeline.tsx, every stage row (`StageRow`, lines ~144-209) renders with IDENTICAL visual weight regardless of stage -- `input`, `correlate`, `risk`, `investigate`, and `decide` all get the same plain icon+badge+headline treatment -- whereas TraceTimeline.tsx's `DecisionStep` (lines ~96-198) gives the terminal decision a distinct 2px-bordered, tinted, larger card. The story's climax (`decide`) is NOT visually distinguished in the artifact analysts read first (the Timeline tab), only in the artifact they read second/rarely (the Trace tab).
- In StageTimeline.tsx there is currently no delta/elapsed-time indicator between consecutive stages (only an absolute `formatTimestamp(stage.ts)` per stage) -- so an analyst cannot see at a glance whether triage took 3 seconds or 3 hours between stages, even though the backend already computes MTTA/MTTR/dwell percentiles elsewhere (`engine/metrics.py`).
- The 'trusted vs untrusted' fencing (#9) in both StageTimeline and TraceTimeline is currently signaled ONLY by a badge/icon (`Lock` icon + 'untrusted' text + switching from `<Markdown>` to `<CodeBlock>`) -- there is no consistent structural/border cue that lets a reader visually scan down the page and separate 'our trusted narrative voice' zones from 'raw untrusted payload' zones without reading every badge.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Add a BLUF card at the very top of the Story surface (before the stage `<ol>` in StageTimeline.tsx) -- reuse the existing VerdictBadge/StatusBadge/ConfidenceBadge + decision-by badge already built for WhyPanel.tsx (lines 114-130) and hoist a 1-2 sentence plain-English summary (`case.summary` or `rationale.decision_rationale` truncated) above the fold. Today that content only appears after clicking the separate 'Why' tab; an analyst opening the case should see the bottom line within the first screen, not after a tab-switch.
- Give the `decide` stage row in StageTimeline.tsx's `StageRow` the SAME visual treatment `DecisionStep` already has in TraceTimeline.tsx (`rounded-xl border-2 border-low/40 bg-low/5 p-4`, larger 8x8 icon badge, 'Deterministic decision' Badge) instead of the generic `pb-6` div all other stages use (lines ~172-206). This is a ~15-line, low-risk change (special-case `stage.kind === 'decide'` inside the existing component) that makes the story's climax pop the way it already does in the less-visited Trace tab.
- Compute and render a duration-delta chip between consecutive stages in `StageTimeline` (e.g. `+3s`, `+1m42s`) by diffing `stages[i].ts - stages[i-1].ts` in the root component and passing it as a prop into `StageRow`; render it inline next to the existing `formatTimestamp(stage.ts)` span (line ~184). This turns the timeline from 'a list of facts' into 'a pace-of-response story,' matching Sentinel's and PagerDuty's emphasis on visible elapsed time, and is cheap to compute client-side from data you already have.
- Add a non-color-alone 'voice' marker to each stage headline: prefix deterministic stages with verbs like 'Detected', 'Correlated', 'Matched policy' and prefix the `investigate` stage headline (and any LLM-authored `reasoning` text in WhyPanel) with an explicit eyebrow label such as 'AI assessed:' rendered as a small uppercase span before the headline (`stage.headline`, StageTimeline.tsx line ~189). Pair this with a distinct rail-dot color for the `investigate` stage specifically (not just the existing per-kind `TONE_RING` map) so the fact/assessment split is legible both by icon+color (already present) AND by language (currently absent) -- reinforces #9 without touching the fencing logic itself.
- Give untrusted blocks a structural cue beyond the badge: add a `border-l-2 border-warning/60` (or your existing warning token) to the wrapping `<div>` around every `<CodeBlock>` rendering of untrusted content in StepItem (StageTimeline.tsx ~line 135) and ReactStep (TraceTimeline.tsx ~line 268-276), so a reader scanning down the page can visually parse 'trusted narrative zone' vs 'raw untrusted payload zone' the way a pull-quote rule separates voices in a printed report, without reading every badge.
- Default-expand the stages that carry the actual assessment content and default-collapse the mechanical ones: in StageTimeline.tsx's `StageRow`, the `Collapsible`'s `open` state currently defaults to `false` for every stage uniformly (line ~151, `useState(false)`); change the default to `true` for `investigate` and `decide` (where the reasoning/verdict live) and keep `input`/`correlate`/`risk`/`triage` collapsed by default -- this applies BLUF/inverted-pyramid ordering (front-load the payoff) inside the per-stage disclosure, not just at the page level.
- Reduce the tab-fragmentation of the same underlying story: either (a) merge `timeline` + `why` into one 'Story' tab in CaseDetail.tsx (currently 8 separate `TabsTrigger`s at lines ~1327-1351) with the BLUF card + StageTimeline as the primary read and WhyPanel's knowledge/tools/enrichment/MITRE cards appended below the stage list as 'supporting detail', or (b) at minimum add explicit cross-links -- a 'See full agent trace ->' button at the bottom of StageTimeline that calls the existing `setTab('trace')` handler, and a jump-link from WhyPanel's header back to the Story. Ship (b) first (near-zero risk, reuses existing `setTab` plumbing already visible at CaseDetail.tsx lines ~1209/1224), treat (a) as the larger Round-7 IA move once you're ready to touch the tab contract.
- Pin the case's primary entity (the existing `Case.entity: {type, value}` field in backend/app/models.py) as a small persistent chip at the top of the Story tab, next to the BLUF card -- gives the reader the 'who/what' anchor before the 'what happened' prose, mirroring Sentinel's co-located Entities widget. Do NOT invent a multi-entity list; the schema only carries one entity per case today (clustering is single-entity-keyed), so keep this to the one chip you actually have data for.
- Adopt the 'Martini Glass' structure explicitly as your Story-tab contract: force strict linear top-to-bottom stage order with no jump navigation for first-time reading (already true), then let 'Show N steps' / 'Show more' progressive-disclosure controls (already implemented in StageTimeline's `Collapsible` and `StepItem`) be the ONLY way to branch into free-form detail. This validates your current architecture -- don't add competing free-form graph/entity-pivot views to the primary Story read; keep those (Threat context tab, Trace tab, Chat) as the 'bowl' a reader opts into after the linear stem.
- If/when you add a mini in-page stage-jump nav (a horizontal breadcrumb of the 6 stage icons, sticky at the top of the Story tab) for long investigations, treat it as a 'comic-strip' overview map (Segel & Heer genre), not a competing navigation model -- clicking a stage icon should scroll-to + auto-expand that stage's `Collapsible`, not replace the linear read.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Do not let the 'full audit trail' (raw ReAct spans / tool calls / tokens / cost) compete with the plain-English narrative for primary reading position -- every best-in-class competitor (Dropzone, Prophet, Sentinel) keeps the audit trail as a secondary, opt-in artifact, not the first thing an analyst sees.
- Do not rely on color alone to separate SIEM-asserted fact from AI-derived assessment -- WCAG and the SOC-alert-triage literature both call out that severity-tier color alone causes bulk-dismissal blindness; pair every color cue with an icon AND a language cue (verb choice).
- Do not bury the verdict/BLUF behind a tab click -- every intelligence-writing and AI-SOC-report convention researched puts the bottom line in the first screen, not the second tab.
- Do not fragment one incident's story across many peer-level tabs with no cross-links -- this reproduces the exact 'context-switch across consoles' cognitive-load problem the alert-fatigue literature identifies as a root cause of missed correlations, just moved inside your own app instead of across vendor consoles.
- Do not give every stage/step identical visual weight -- if the climax (the deterministic decide() step) looks the same as routine ingestion steps, readers lose the story's shape; Segel & Heer's 'visual narrative tactics' explicitly call for distinct highlighting of key moments.
- Do not let 'more transparency' become 'more clutter' -- Dropzone's 'No Black Boxes' pitch is paired with progressive disclosure (ranked, plain-English findings first), not a wall of raw tool output; keep StepItem/ReactStep's existing collapse-by-default behavior for the mechanical stages.
- Do not add a free-form investigation graph/canvas as a competing primary view for the Story tab -- Sentinel itself notes the graph's downside is heavier context-switching; keep graph/entity-pivot exploration as an opt-in secondary tool (matches your existing Threat-context tab), consistent with the Martini Glass model (linear first, free-form second, not simultaneous).

**Sources:**

- https://support.pagerduty.com/main/docs/narrative-builder
- http://vis.stanford.edu/files/2010-Narrative-InfoVis.pdf

## AI transparency / provenance labeling in SOC UIs — distinguishing SIEM-asserted facts, deterministic-code outputs, and AI/LLM-derived assessments


**Key findings:**
- IBM Carbon's "AI label" component (carbondesignsystem.com/components/ai-label) is the most fully-specified pattern for this exact problem: a small, ALWAYS-visible, non-decorative indicator placed directly ON or immediately beside the AI-touched value (never a page-level banner), which opens an "explainability popover" on click/hover. The popover uses progressive disclosure — a one-line "this is AI output" surface, then optional deeper "why/how" layers — instead of dumping everything at once. Carbon explicitly frames the AI label as "a pathway to explainability," not just a sticker.
- C2PA Content Credentials (spec.c2pa.org/.../UX_Recommendations.html) formalizes a 4-level progressive-disclosure model that generalizes well beyond images: L1 = a small, permanently-visible, NEVER-recolored/altered badge that just signals "provenance data exists here"; L2 = hover/click summary (who/what produced it, timestamp, claim generator); L3 = full manifest/history (their own docs call this a "digital nutrition label"); L4 = forensic/raw detail. The badge's visual CONSISTENCY (same icon everywhere, no per-instance styling) is what makes it pattern-matchable at a glance across an entire product — directly applicable to a 3-way SIEM/deterministic/AI tag system.
- Microsoft's 18 Guidelines for Human-AI Interaction (Amershi et al., CHI 2019, microsoft.com/en-us/research) and Microsoft 365 Copilot's citation UI converge on: make confidence/limitations visible AT THE POINT OF USE (inline, not just in a settings page or onboarding tour); attach clickable citations to the SPECIFIC claim they support, plus an aggregated "show all sources" panel for the whole answer — i.e. both inline micro-provenance AND a rollup view are needed, not either alone.
- CORTEX (arXiv 2503.02065, "Too Much to Trust? Measuring the Security and Cognitive Impacts of Explainability in AI-Driven SOCs") is a SOC-analyst-specific study and the single most relevant academic source found: it identifies a non-linear "trust-explainability curve" — too little explanation breeds skepticism, but too much (raw model internals/SHAP values) during time-pressured Tier-1 triage causes either blind deference to an "authoritative-looking" explanation the analyst doesn't actually understand, OR disuse from confusion. It recommends tiering explanation depth by severity AND analyst role/tier: concise driver + 1-2 evidence anchors for fast Tier-1 triage, full timelines/correlations for Tier-2 investigation, model internals only for Tier-3 forensic/tuning work. It also found analysts trusted explanations MORE when they cited historical baselines or prior-confirmed-threat matches than when they showed a bare confidence number alone.
- Elastic AI Assistant's citation feature (elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/ai-assistant) uses hover-superscript citations resolving to a labeled, clickable source (knowledge base entry, Security Labs doc, or user-supplied data) — and Elastic's own engineering discussion flags that naively interpolating citation markers into rendered LLM text is an XSS risk; citations must be rendered as a structured, out-of-band list/array the UI controls, not parsed out of raw model markup.
- Google SecOps Gemini and CrowdStrike Charlotte AI both explicitly avoid presenting the AI's true/false-positive call as a fact: Gemini's classification is described as a "proposed verdict" the analyst reviews/validates (with thumbs-up/down feedback capture), and Charlotte AI pairs its TP/FP call with a confidence score plus "traceable and auditable insights" / "inspectable source data" as the actual trust mechanism — i.e. the trust signal is the AUDITABILITY AND INSPECTABILITY of the evidence chain, not the badge's color or wording.
- Darktrace Cyber AI Analyst's core trust mechanic is a generated natural-language narrative per autonomous decision ("shows its work") that cites the underlying telemetry/timeline it reasoned over — the narrative and the raw telemetry it cites are visually distinct (story vs. evidence), which is the same trusted-prose/untrusted-payload split TLSOC's TraceTimeline already implements for ReAct spans.
- Multiple 2024-2025 empirical studies (arXiv 2506.16202; PMC12887897 "Labeling messages as AI-generated does not reduce their persuasive effects") found that a bare TEXT label ("AI-generated") by itself has limited/inconsistent effect on how much people trust or scrutinize content — sometimes it modestly lowers perceived accuracy, sometimes it does nothing. The actionable implication across these studies is that the label only works as a trust mechanism when it is a functional AFFORDANCE (opens evidence/citations/rationale), not decorative text — consistent with Carbon and C2PA's insistence that the badge always leads to a grounding layer.
- Our own codebase (webui/src/soc/components/TraceTimeline.tsx) has already independently invented two of the three needed provenance idioms — a `trusted` boolean that renders untrusted tool/log payloads only inside a fenced, labeled `<CodeBlock>` (never plain text), and a visually DISTINCT terminal `DecisionStep` card badged "Deterministic decision" with a Lock icon and the exact policy clause `decide()` matched — but this vocabulary is currently confined to the Timeline/Why tab. Everywhere else a value appears (Cases list, CaseHoverCard, OverviewPanel's fact grid, case header), severity/risk/verdict/confidence badges are rendered with the SAME visual grammar (colored pill + severity-style icon) with no indication of which of the three sources produced them, so an analyst scanning a case row cannot tell a raw SIEM `severity` from a deterministic `risk_score` from an LLM `confidence` at a glance — this is the concrete gap Round-7 should close.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Add a fourth, ICON-ONLY (never color-coded) shared primitive `ProvenanceTag`/`SourceGlyph` in `webui/src/soc/components/` (sibling to `badges.tsx`'s existing `SemanticGlyph`), with exactly 3-4 values: `siem` (Database/Server icon), `deterministic` (Lock icon — reuse the exact icon TraceTimeline's DecisionStep already uses), `ai` (Bot icon — reuse the icon already used for `invoke_agent` spans, not a sparkle/magic-wand, to keep tone consistent with a serious security console), and optionally `human` (User icon, already imported elsewhere) for analyst-entered fields. Register the icon map in `palette.ts` as `PROVENANCE_ICON`, following the exact pattern of the existing `SEMANTIC_ICON` single-authority map, so it's centrally authored like severity/status/verdict rather than ad-hoc per component.
- Do NOT give provenance its own color. The app already has 3 orthogonal color axes (severity/status/verdict per palette.ts); a 4th color channel for provenance would compete with them and violate the project's own 'meaning is never color-only' rule. Encode provenance via icon + a 1-2 word label only (e.g. a tiny outline chip reading 'SIEM' / 'Deterministic' / 'AI' with the matching glyph), rendered in neutral/muted styling regardless of the value's severity color.
- Wire `ProvenanceTag` at these exact spots, using a static field→source lookup table (this is a UI-only, zero-backend-change change — the mapping is fixed by which module produces the field): OverviewPanel.tsx fact grid — severity, rule name, host/user/source_ip, timestamps, raw OCSF fields → `siem`; risk_score, priority, MITRE coverage %, threshold/baseline anomaly flags, and any `decision_by === 'case_manager'` status → `deterministic`; verdict, confidence, investigator reasoning excerpt, enrichment interpretation, campaign 'why grouped' text → `ai`.
- In WhyPanel.tsx, generalize TraceTimeline's bespoke 'Deterministic decision' Lock treatment into the shared ProvenanceTag component so the same icon/copy is reused everywhere instead of being one-off to that card — and add the SAME one-line disclaimer TraceTimeline already writes ('the close/escalate decision is made by deterministic code against the operator-configured policy — never by raw model output') as a hover tooltip on every `deterministic`-tagged StatusBadge/VerdictBadge combo anywhere it appears (Cases list, CaseHoverCard, case header) — today that trust statement is buried in the Timeline tab only, three clicks deep.
- Make every ProvenanceTag a real affordance, not decoration (per the 2024-2025 labeling-effectiveness research): wrap it in the Popover/HoverCard primitive already used elsewhere (e.g. CaseHoverCard) so clicking an `ai` tag opens a short popover with model name + timestamp + confidence + a 'See rationale' link to the Why tab/`/api/cases/{id}/rationale`, and clicking a `siem` tag opens/links to the raw event's untrusted `CodeBlock` view. A bare text label with no click-through will underperform per the cited labeling studies.
- Match tag density to view density: use the FULL icon+label ProvenanceTag chip in card/detail views (OverviewPanel, CaseHoverCard, story view) where there's room and values are adjacent and easily confused; in the dense Cases list DataTable, bake a single small persistent glyph into the column HEADER instead of per-row chips (e.g. 'Verdict' column header carries a small Bot glyph, 'Risk' column header carries a small Lock glyph) — per-row chips across hundreds of rows would be visual noise CORTEX's 'trust-explainability curve' finding warns against for time-pressured triage.
- For the case 'story' view specifically: structure it as three visually distinct blocks — 'What the source reported' (siem, plain read-only facts), 'What the system decided' (deterministic, framed with the Lock idiom + policy clause, reusing DecisionStep's card treatment), and 'What the AI assessed' (ai, framed inside a consistent neutral/info-toned bordered container — reuse the existing `border-info/40 bg-info/10` token already used for TraceTimeline info-tone steps — so AI-assessed content always sits inside the same visual 'frame' site-wide regardless of the case's own severity color). Tag once at the SECTION header level here rather than per-field, since the three blocks are already spatially separated.
- For the Noise-Reduction funnel infographic: label each funnel node (Ingest → Correlate → Risk-score → Router(cheap LLM) → Investigator(strong LLM) → decide()) with the same 3 ProvenanceTag icons so the funnel visibly ALTERNATES siem → deterministic → ai → ai → deterministic. This turns the architecture itself into a differentiating trust story (unlike an end-to-end black-box AI triage product, TLSOC has deterministic gates before AND after the model) and should be called out explicitly in the infographic's caption/legend.
- Add lightweight inline source citations to the investigator reasoning text already surfaced in WhyPanel: since `CaseRationale` already carries `knowledge[]` (RAG/runbook snippets), `enrichment[]`, and `tools[]` (commands/queries run) as separate arrays, render a small 'Sources referenced' footer row under the reasoning paragraph listing which of those entries backed it (e.g. 'RAG: runbook-lateral-movement · Enrichment: abuseipdb · Tool: es_query'), each linking down to the matching item already displayed elsewhere in the same panel — mirroring MS365 Copilot's inline-citation + 'show all sources' pattern and Elastic's superscript citations. Do NOT implement this by parsing citation markers out of the LLM's raw text or rendering it as HTML — build the reasoning text and the citation list as two structurally separate, React-rendered arrays (per #9 fencing and to avoid the markup-injection risk Elastic's own engineering flagged for naive citation interpolation).
- Add a lightweight per-case analyst feedback affordance next to the AI-assessed block (thumbs up/down or 'agree/disagree with verdict', already partially present via `Case.feedback`/`POST /api/cases/{id}/feedback` per the existing feedback loop) directly inside the ai-provenance frame, mirroring Google SecOps Gemini's per-summary feedback capture — this closes the loop CORTEX identifies as necessary for trust calibration over time, and TLSOC already has the backend endpoint, so this is a UI-wiring task, not new plumbing.
- Keep provenance copy neutral/professional, not hedging or alarmist ('AI-assessed', 'Computed by policy', 'Source-reported') rather than anything implying unreliability ('AI (unverified!)') — per the labeling-effectiveness research, tone matters: labels that read as an apology or disclaimer measurably lower perceived trust more than a neutral factual tag does, even for identical underlying content.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Don't add a 4th color-coded semantic axis for provenance — the app already has 3 orthogonal color axes (severity/status/verdict) authored as a single palette.ts authority; a colored 'provenance' pill would compete with and muddy those, and violates the project's existing non-color-only accessibility rule. Use icon+label only.
- Don't rely on a single global banner/disclaimer ('This case was AI-triaged') at the top of the page as the whole transparency mechanism — both Carbon and C2PA explicitly place their indicator ON/NEAR the specific content it explains, and 2024-2025 labeling research found generic disclaimer-style labels have weak, inconsistent effects on trust calibration compared to inline, click-through provenance.
- Don't show bare confidence decimals (e.g. '73.4%') as the only signal — CORTEX and multiple UX-pattern writeups found raw probabilities are ambiguous to analysts without a task-oriented frame or a stated driver ('why this number'); TLSOC's existing banded ConfidenceBadge (Low/Med/High + %) is the right shape, but should gain a 'why' link into the rationale rather than stopping at the number.
- Don't over-explain during time-pressured Tier-1 triage — CORTEX found dumping full model internals/SHAP-style detail on every alert either causes blind deference to an authoritative-looking explanation the analyst can't evaluate, or confusion-driven disuse. Default views should show a compact tag + one-line driver; full detail should be progressive-disclosure (click-through to Why tab), matching C2PA's L1→L4 layering.
- Don't render untrusted SIEM/log-derived text or AI reasoning as interpolated HTML/markup when adding citations or provenance popovers — Elastic's own engineering flagged this exact XSS surface for citation rendering. Keep citations and provenance metadata as structured arrays rendered by React components, consistent with TLSOC's existing #9 fencing (CodeBlock for untrusted payloads, plain text for trusted prose) — never parse markers out of raw LLM output into markup.
- Don't let per-row provenance chips add visual noise to dense list views (Cases table, hundreds of rows) — that density mismatch is exactly what CORTEX's trust-explainability curve warns against; push provenance to the column-header level in tables and reserve full chips for card/detail views.
- Don't use hedging/alarmist copy ('AI (unverified)', 'may be wrong!') as the label text — labeling-effectiveness studies show tone affects perceived trust independent of underlying accuracy; use neutral, factual, professional phrasing consistent with a serious security console (this also matches Carbon's plain-text 'AI' label choice over a decorative sparkle/magic-wand icon, which reads as marketing rather than a trust signal in a SOC context).
- Don't ship the badge without a functional click-through — the strongest common thread across Carbon's 'explainability popover', C2PA's L2 disclosure, and the 2024-2025 labeling-effectiveness papers is that a label with no path to grounding evidence has weak or even negative trust effects; every provenance tag must open (popover or link) to the actual evidence/rationale, not just announce a category.

**Sources:**

## Human-in-the-loop AI feedback UX: folding "was the AI right?" grading into the case-close/action moment (not a separate form) — for the TLSOC Round-7 overhaul


**Key findings:**
- CrowdStrike Charlotte AI: Falcon Complete analysts 'continuously review, validate, and score' verdicts DURING real casework, not via a bolted-on survey — the scoring is a byproduct of normal case handling, feeding an 'accuracy flywheel' (claimed 98% triage accuracy). Source: crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/inside-the-human-ai-feedback-loop-powering-crowdstrike-agentic-security
- Swimlane's tiered-confidence pattern: >=98% confidence auto-closes with no ask; ~90% confidence presents the verdict for 'one-click confirmation' — the single confirm click IS the feedback record; only low-confidence cases get a full manual investigation. Confidence level should set the friction of the grading UI. Source: swimlane.com/blog/verdict-ai-agent
- Prophet Security: 'analyst feedback is incorporated at the investigation and step level' (granular, in-context) rather than end-of-case surveys; corrections carry an analyst-set scope + expiration so stale corrections don't linger. Source: prophetsecurity.ai/ai-soc-analyst
- Google Chronicle SOAR's Close Case dialog already puts a required Disposition/root-cause single-select (True Positive/False Positive/Benign Positive) INSIDE the close action itself — the industry-standard 'grading lives at close time' pattern, though Chronicle doesn't yet compare it against an AI verdict. Source: docs.cloud.google.com/chronicle/docs/soar/investigate/working-with-cases/how-to-close-cases and .../custom-fields-in-case-closure
- Microsoft Copilot Studio attaches a thumbs-up/down + optional comment ('Reactions') to literally every AI response, not a survey — proves the 'one-tap rating stapled to the artifact you're already looking at' pattern works at scale; a separate 'Custom metrics' layer adds structured signal beyond thumbs. Source: learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-copilot-studio/analytics-improve-agent-effectiveness
- Microsoft HAX Guideline 15 'Encourage granular feedback': rate the INDIVIDUAL output, in the moment, not the whole system; explicitly recommends reusing existing interaction/acceptance data as implicit feedback rather than always asking. Source: microsoft.com/en-us/haxtoolkit/guideline/encourage-granular-feedback
- Microsoft HAX Guideline 9 'Support efficient correction' (pattern 9A 'switch classification decisions'): let the user directly reclassify the AI's output in place — this is exactly what an unlocked Disposition dropdown at close time already does; don't add a parallel correction control.
- Google PAIR 'Design AI feedback loops' and 'Errors + Graceful Failure': feedback must be low-friction and contextual, and must visibly close the loop (show the user their input changed something) or it becomes a 'feedback black hole' analysts stop using; the error/override moment is explicitly called out as the highest-value place to capture calibration data.
- Automation-bias research (MDPI 2025 SOC study; Springer AI & Society 2025 review): explicit reasoning transparency + accountability raise 'appropriate reliance'; an 'evaluative AI' framing (show evidence for/against rather than one recommended label) reduces both over- and under-reliance — argues against a UI that just asks 'agree?' next to the AI's own pre-highlighted answer.
- Contestability research (arXiv 2506.01662, 'Explainable AI Systems Must Be Contestable'): distinguishes descriptive 'explanation' from normative 'justification' — an override should capture WHY (justification), not just a corrected label, to be useful as audit/training data.
- Calibration-UX research (arXiv 2402.07632, 2202.05983): a bare confidence number doesn't improve trust calibration by itself; showing the AI's actual historical accuracy at that confidence band alongside the ask gives analysts something concrete to grade against instead of grading blind.
- No vendor blog we could reach (CrowdStrike, Elastic, Unit21, Dropzone) publishes the literal pixel-level UI of their grading control — the concrete mechanics above had to be inferred from HAX/PAIR guideline patterns plus SOAR precedent (Chronicle); the strongest evidence is the DESIGN PRINCIPLE (fold it into the action, keep it granular/low-friction, close the loop), not a single reference screenshot.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Merge grading into webui/src/soc/pages/casedetail/ConfirmActionDialog.tsx instead of leaving it solely in the separate webui/src/soc/pages/casedetail/FeedbackPanel.tsx tab. Add a new ActionField (e.g. 'ai_grade') to the union in shared.tsx (~line 62) and attach it to close_disposition's `fields` so the grading strip renders inside the SAME dialog the analyst already uses to pick Disposition — not a second destination.
- Do not add a standalone 'Agree/Disagree' button next to the Disposition select — derive it automatically. The moment the analyst picks a DISPOSITION_OPTIONS value (already required; submit is already gated on `!disposition` per ConfirmActionDialog.tsx line 234), diff it client-side against `case.verdict` via a small map (TRUE_POSITIVE<->'true_positive'; FALSE_POSITIVE<->{'false_positive','benign'}; NEEDS_HUMAN<->{'suspicious','undetermined'}; 'duplicate' excluded) and render a live inline badge: 'Matches AI verdict' / 'Overrides AI verdict (False Positive -> True Positive)'. This captures FeedbackEntry.assessment (agree/partial/disagree) at zero extra clicks and avoids the automation-bias trap of a pre-anchored confirm button — preserve the current 'no default selection, forced active choice' behavior of the Disposition select; do not regress it by pre-filling the AI's own verdict.
- Only when the derived comparison is a MISMATCH, reveal one optional single-line input inline: 'What did the AI miss? (optional)', feeding FeedbackEntry.comment. Keep the existing 3-star block (accuracy/reasoning_quality/action_appropriateness in FeedbackPanel.tsx) OUT of the default close flow; collapse it behind a 'Rate in detail ->' disclosure inside the same dialog for analysts/QA who want the fuller grading, so a routine close stays a 2-second action.
- In CaseDetail.tsx's runAction handler (~line 809-815), when `case.verdict` is set, fire `api.caseFeedback(caseId, {assessment: derivedAssessment, actual_outcome: disposition, comment: overrideReason})` as a non-blocking side effect alongside the existing action POST (which keeps posting the unchanged `close`/`resolve` wire verb through the real decide()/apply(), #3-intact). Skip the feedback call entirely when there's no verdict to grade (e.g. NEEDS_HUMAN cases closed without ever getting a verdict) or when the dialog is cancelled — no partial/silent writes.
- Retire the redundant `OUTCOME_OPTIONS` dropdown in FeedbackPanel.tsx — it asks the same true_positive/false_positive/benign/etc. question the close dialog's Disposition select already asked, in a different vocabulary, in a different place, creating a chance the two answers contradict. Instead, have the Feedback tab open pre-seeded from `case.disposition`/the derived assessment already recorded on the case, and let it serve purely as the 'add detail later / QA re-review' surface, not the first-touch grading surface.
- Branch the inline grading strip's copy on `case.verdict`: for TRUE_POSITIVE/FALSE_POSITIVE show 'Was the AI right?' tied to the disposition match/mismatch; for NEEDS_HUMAN, skip agree/disagree entirely (there's no verdict to grade) and ask only 'Was the investigation useful?' with a single reasoning-quality star — avoid one grading UI forced across all three verdict classes.
- Add a confidence-band calibration anchor next to the grading strip: bucket `case.confidence` into deciles and, server-side in engine/metrics.py, join against disposition-vs-verdict match rate across ALL closed cases (see next item) to show e.g. 'AI was right ~91% of the time at this confidence level (last 30d)'. This gives analysts a track record to grade against instead of grading blind, per the calibration-UX research above.
- Compute an IMPLICIT, whole-fleet agreement signal in engine/metrics.py::feedback_stats (or a sibling function) from `case.verdict` vs `case.disposition` across every closed/resolved case, independent of whether an explicit FeedbackEntry exists. Today feedback_stats only aggregates the small, self-selecting subset of cases where an analyst separately visited the Feedback tab — almost certainly a low-n, non-representative sample (likely skewed toward strong disagreement, since happy-path closes have no reason to visit a 3rd tab). Report the implicit disposition-vs-verdict match rate as the Metrics/dashboard headline calibration number, and keep the richer explicit FeedbackEntry stats (stars/comments) as a clearly-labeled secondary 'n=X detailed gradings' metric — don't let the opt-in sample masquerade as the whole-SOC number.
- Add a reliability/calibration-curve widget to Metrics.tsx (and optionally the Security Command Center dashboard): x-axis = AI confidence decile, y-axis = actual agreement rate in that decile (from the implicit signal above), with a 45-degree reference line; flag deciles where the AI is over-confident (agreement well below stated confidence). Follow the dataviz skill for the palette/accessibility pass on this chart before building it.
- Add a one-line 'this feeds nightly threshold tuning' HelpTip under the inline grading strip, consistent with the existing engine/threshold_tuner.py + AutomationNudge machinery already in the codebase, and surface a lightweight in-app notification when accumulated overrides actually trigger a tuning change (e.g. 'Your feedback on 12 cases contributed to a threshold tune on rule X') via the existing notifications/in-app inbox — this is the concrete 'close the loop, don't create a feedback black hole' fix for our product specifically.
- Place the grading strip directly beneath the existing verdict/confidence chip block in OverviewPanel.tsx rather than lower in the dialog or on a separate tab, so the SIEM-facts-vs-AI-assessment visual separation already used in the case story view carries through into the exact moment the analyst is asked to grade the assessment layer.
- Add regression tests mirroring the existing FeedbackPanel.test.tsx / CaseDetail.tabs.test.tsx patterns: assert that closing a verdict-bearing case always issues both the action POST and the feedback POST in one submit, that cancelling the dialog issues neither, and that the backend's case_feedback route (routes.py ~line 3196) tolerates a minimal payload (assessment + actual_outcome only, no stars) since that will now be the common case.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- A separate 'Feedback' tab/form the analyst must remember to visit after acting kills participation rate — this is literally our current FeedbackTab.tsx design, and it is exactly the anti-pattern this research topic was scoped to fix.
- Asking the same TP/FP/benign question twice in two vocabularies (our Disposition select at close vs. the Feedback tab's separate actual_outcome select) doubles the ask for zero benefit and risks the two answers disagreeing.
- Overloading the close moment with a full 3-star-rating-plus-comment form turns a 2-second action into a 30-second one, which will get rage-clicked-through or will visibly slow case-closure throughput, a real SOC KPI — default to one derived signal plus one optional field, not the whole rich form, every time.
- Asking for feedback AFTER the action is already committed (a follow-up toast, a separate page) has far lower completion than asking inside the same still-open dialog/submit — don't split the moment across two round-trips.
- Pre-selecting or visually anchoring the AI's own verdict as the 'default' answer in a grading/confirm control nudges analysts toward rubber-stamping (automation bias) — keep the Disposition select unset-by-default (as it already is) and derive agreement from the analyst's own independent choice rather than asking them to also click a redundant 'I agree' next to a pre-highlighted answer.
- A one-size-fits-all rating UI across TRUE_POSITIVE/FALSE_POSITIVE/NEEDS_HUMAN verdicts is a mismatch — there is nothing to 'agree/disagree' with on a NEEDS_HUMAN case since the AI made no call; forcing the same widget there confuses analysts and pollutes the calibration metric.
- Free-text-only 'Comments' boxes with no specific prompt go unused — frame the optional field narrowly ('What did the AI miss?') and only surface it on an actual mismatch, not on every close.
- Not closing the loop (never showing the analyst that their disagreement/correction changed a threshold, rule, or model behavior) causes explicit feedback to trail off over time — PAIR's 'feedback black hole' failure mode; our backend already has the tuning/automation machinery to close this loop, the UI just needs to surface it.
- Treating a low-n, self-selected explicit-feedback sample as if it represents the whole SOC's calibration (the current feedback_stats behavior) overstates or understates AI accuracy on the dashboard — always report the implicit whole-fleet signal as the headline number and label the explicit-survey number as a smaller supplementary sample.

**Sources:**
- https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/inside-the-human-ai-feedback-loop-powering-crowdstrike-agentic-security/
- https://swimlane.com/blog/verdict-ai-agent/
- https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/alert-triage-agentic-soc-elastic-workflows
- https://www.prophetsecurity.ai/ai-soc-analyst
- https://www.prophetsecurity.ai/
- https://www.dropzone.ai/product
- https://www.dropzone.ai/resource-guide/ai-soc-analysts-the-complete-guide-to-alert-management
- https://www.unit21.ai/blog/ai-agents-for-case-investigations-what-changes-and-what-stays-the-same
- https://torq.io/blog/ai-alert-triage/
- https://docs.cloud.google.com/chronicle/docs/soar/investigate/working-with-cases/how-to-close-cases
- https://docs.cloud.google.com/chronicle/docs/soar/investigate/working-with-cases/custom-fields-in-case-closure
- https://docs.cloud.google.com/chronicle/docs/soar/investigate/working-with-cases/add-new-case-close-root-cause-admin

## Data-dense table/list design for security case queues — scannability, row density, badge usage, sticky headers, column prioritization, progressive disclosure


**Key findings:**
- Row density is a named, PERSISTED user preference in mature systems, not one hardcoded value. Carbon Design System ships Compact/Default/Tall row heights selectable from a table settings menu; Setproduct's 2026 data-table guide frames it as Compact/Comfortable/Spacious and states 'density is a feature, not a default — the right row height is the one each user selected.' Converged pixel guidance: ~40-44px compact / 48-56px comfortable rows, 16px horizontal cell padding, 8-12px vertical padding, 1px hairline row separators. (Pencil & Paper: https://www.pencilandpaper.io/articles/ux-pattern-analysis-enterprise-data-tables; Setproduct: https://www.setproduct.com/blog/data-table-ui-design)
- Zebra striping is an ANTI-PATTERN once a table also has hover/selected/disabled states — Pencil & Paper notes combining zebra + hover + selected + disabled produces '5 semantic levels of grey' that reads as noise; a 1px hairline row-divider + hover highlight scans better at high row counts. NN Group's four-task framework agrees: borders + hover-triggered row highlighting (not stripes) is what lets a user keep their place scanning many rows.
- Sticky/frozen table headers are considered mandatory once row count exceeds one viewport (NN Group video 'Designing Tables for Desktop Apps with Lots of Data'; Carbon Design System GitHub issue #1587 'Header should be sticky'; Setproduct 2026 guide: 'a sticky header becomes mandatory the moment a table is tall enough that column titles scroll out of view'). Implementation detail that matters: the sticky header needs an OPAQUE background (not translucent) so scrolled rows don't bleed through, and in an app with its OWN persistent top chrome, the table header must stick to `top: <app-header-height>`, not `top: 0` — otherwise it slides invisibly underneath the app nav instead of parking just beneath it.
- Column prioritization is decision-driven, not alphabetical or DB-column-order. NN Group's 'Data Tables: Four Major User Tasks' (https://www.nngroup.com/articles/data-tables/) says: lead with a human-readable identifier (not an internal ID) in column 1, order remaining columns by how often a user consults that value when deciding what to do next, and group related fields adjacently to cut eye travel per row. Both NN Group and Pencil & Paper stress the DEFAULT on-load column set should be a product-curated MINIMUM needed to triage/decide, with show/hide/reorder/resize as a separate user-level customization layer on top — not 'ship every column visible and let users manually prune it.'
- Best-in-class SOC/XDR incident queues collapse many contributing signals into ONE dominant priority/severity value rather than showing 5-7 separate scored axes as competing columns. Microsoft Defender XDR's incident queue (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/defender-xdr/incident-queue) assigns ONE 0-100 priority score via its 'Queue Assistant' ML ranker (inputs: attack-disruption signals, threat analytics, severity, SNR, MITRE techniques, asset criticality, alert rarity — all fused into one number), color-coded in just 3 tiers (red >85 / orange 15-85 / gray <15), with the CONTRIBUTING FACTORS only revealed in a side summary pane opened by clicking the row — i.e. progressive disclosure of the 'why', not a wall of badges in the row itself. Datadog's Cloud SIEM Signals Explorer (https://docs.datadoghq.com/security/cloud_siem/triage_and_investigate/investigate_security_signals/) similarly keeps the list to Severity + Signal State (Open/Under Review/Archived) as the scan columns, with everything else opened on click.
- Status/severity indicators must never be color-only. PatternFly's status-and-severity pattern (https://www.patternfly.org/patterns/status-and-severity/) and Red Hat's badge accessibility guidance (https://ux.redhat.com/elements/badge/accessibility/) both require pairing color with an icon AND a text label, keeping a consistent severity ordering (their reference scale: Critical > Important > Moderate > Minor > None/Undefined, 3-point minimum), and NEVER swapping which icon/color goes with which meaning row-to-row. General accessibility guidance (https://www.accessibility.chat/articles/when-color-coding-fails-why-status-indicators-need-more-than-pretty-colors) adds: avoid red/green pairing (most common colorblind confusion — ~8% of men), prefer blue/orange contrasts, and maintain ≥3:1 non-text contrast between the indicator and its background.
- Progressive disclosure in dense tables follows a small fixed vocabulary of patterns, chosen by how much detail is needed: inline expandable rows / tree-tables for structurally nested data; a hover tooltip or hover-card for a 'sneak peek' without leaving the list; a slide-in drawer/side-panel (NOT a full-page navigation) for the common 'click a row to see everything' case — this is literally what Microsoft's incident queue does (row click → summary pane with priority factors + recommended actions); and a separate drill-down view only when nesting gets deep. (Pencil & Paper: 'the sidebar is the most scalable option... for substantial detail + subtabs.')
- Truncation should default to single-line + ellipsis + a tooltip/hover-card revealing the full value on hover AND keyboard focus — never silent clipping with no way to see the full string, and never letting a long value wrap and break row-height rhythm (Setproduct, Pencil & Paper both flag multi-line wrapped cells as destroying the vertical scan rhythm that makes a dense table fast to read).
- Numeric columns (scores, counts, confidence %) should use tabular/monospace figures so digits align vertically down the column — Pencil & Paper specifically calls out proportional fonts making '$1,111.11' visually smaller than '$999.99' at a glance, which defeats fast top-to-bottom magnitude scanning in a risk/severity/confidence column.
- For very large queues (thousands of rows), client-side rendering of a fully materialized `<table>` starts to stutter past roughly ~1,000 rows (Setproduct); the standard mitigation is server-side pagination/filtering or row virtualization (TanStack Virtual / react-window), and TanStack's own guidance warns that CSS `transform`-based virtualization fights native `<table>` layout math and BREAKS sticky headers — virtualized tables should use spacer rows, not transforms, if a sticky header must survive (https://tanstack.com/virtual/v3/docs/framework/react/examples/sticky, https://github.com/TanStack/virtual/discussions/872).

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Wire the ALREADY-BUILT `sticky` prop on `TableHeader` (webui/src/ui/table.tsx) into the shared `DataTable` primitive (webui/src/soc/components/DataTable.tsx line ~369, `<TableHeader>` with no `sticky` passed today). This capability exists and is tested (webui/src/ui/__tests__/table.test.tsx) but is currently DEAD CODE from the Cases queue's perspective — no page turns it on. Turning it on for Cases/Audit/Cost is a one-line, zero-new-dep win.
- Fix the sticky-header offset before shipping it: `TableHeader`'s sticky class is hardcoded `sticky top-0 z-10`. AppShell's own top bar is `sticky top-0 z-30 h-14` (webui/src/soc/AppShell.tsx:531). If DataTable's header goes sticky as-is, it will sit at `top:0` but be z-ordered BELOW the app bar, so on scroll it disappears behind the nav instead of parking beneath it. Change the sticky offset to `top-[var(--header-h)]` (the same CSS var the codebase already uses for `scroll-mt-[var(--header-h)]` on clickable rows) so the column header sticks directly under the persistent app chrome.
- Collapse the Cases table's 7 overlapping 'how bad/how sure' columns — Severity, Severity (AI), Risk, Confidence, Verdict, Disposition, Urgency (webui/src/soc/pages/Cases.tsx columns array, ~lines 884-930) — into ONE dominant Priority signal for the default row view, following the Defender XDR pattern (one 0-100 score, 3-tier color, contributing factors revealed on click). The backend already computes this: `engine/priority.py` (read-time severity/impact/urgency/priority derivation, advisory-only, never feeds `decide()`) — surface `Case.priority` (or a client-composed equivalent) as the PRIMARY badge/column, and move the other 6 axes into the existing `CaseHoverCard` / `CaseDetail` 'Why' tab (already built) rather than 7 parallel badge columns fighting for attention.
- Curate the Cases table's DEFAULT visible column set down from the current 17 (Case ID, Title, Status, Disposition, Alerts, Playbooks, Enrichments, Category, Severity, Severity(AI), Confidence, Verdict, Risk, Urgency, Entity, Updated, Actions) to ~7-8 decision columns (Case ID, Title, Priority, Status, Entity, Assignee if present, Updated, Actions), with the rest pre-populated into `ColumnState.hidden` by default rather than requiring every user to manually prune via the `ColumnsMenu` (already built, webui/src/soc/components/ColumnsMenu.tsx). Consider per-role default column sets the same way Round-6 already ships 'curated per-role default layouts' for custom dashboards.
- Actually USE the `rowAccent` prop that `DataTable` already implements (webui/src/soc/components/DataTable.tsx lines 162, 460-483: an inset 3px left-edge severity color band, decorative + non-color-redundant, zero layout shift) — it is fully built, documented, and accessibility-reviewed, but `grep` shows it is invoked NOWHERE outside DataTable.tsx itself. Wire `rowAccent={(c) => c.priority ?? c.risk_score ?? caseSeverity(c)}` on the Cases DataTable instance so severity is scannable down the left margin without reading any cell text — this is the single highest-leverage, lowest-effort recommendation in this research because the code already exists and is dormant.
- Add `tabular-nums` to the numeric badges that currently lack it — `RiskBadge` (`{label} {rounded}`) and `ConfidenceBadge` (`fmtPercent(confidence)`) in webui/src/soc/components/badges.tsx render plain proportional digits today, so a column of risk scores (e.g. 72 vs 108) doesn't align vertically for fast magnitude scanning. `DataTable`'s own pager footer already uses `tabular-nums` (line ~531) — extend that convention into the badges.
- Add a sticky-left (frozen) treatment for the Case ID column when the table is wide/many columns are visible — it's already `lockVisible: true` (can't be hidden) but has no horizontal-scroll pinning. Extend `DataTableColumn` with an optional `pin?: 'left'` that applies `sticky left-0 z-[1] bg-card` to that column's th/td, mirroring the pattern already used for the sticky header, so identity survives horizontal scroll on a 15+ column table on a laptop screen.
- Keep (do not regress) three things the codebase already does RIGHT and that match best practice: (1) every semantic badge already pairs color with an icon (`SEMANTIC_ICON`) AND text, never color-only (matches PatternFly/Red Hat a11y guidance exactly); (2) `aria-sort` is applied ONLY to the active sortable column with a redundant live-region announcement (exceeds most SIEM competitors' accessibility); (3) `CaseHoverCard` on the Title/Case-ID cells already implements the 'hover for full preview without leaving the list' pattern NN Group/Pencil&Paper recommend. Cite these as strengths so the Round-7 overhaul doesn't accidentally undo them while chasing new density work.
- For the 'Noise Reduction' funnel/queue at scale: the Cases page currently loads a hard-capped 200 rows client-side and filters/sorts entirely in-browser (`LIST_LIMIT = 200`, `applyFilters`/`sortComparators` in Cases.tsx) — fine today, but flag it as the first thing to swap for server-side pagination/filtering (or `@tanstack/react-virtual` with spacer-row virtualization, NOT transform-based, to keep the new sticky header working) once real deployments exceed roughly 1,000 open+recent cases, per Setproduct's 1,000-row client-rendering ceiling.
- When building the case-story 'SIEM-asserted facts vs AI-derived assessment' separation elsewhere in this overhaul, keep that same fact/assessment split visible at the LIST level too: e.g. keep `Severity` (source/SIEM-asserted) visually distinct in weight/style from `Severity (AI)`/`Verdict`/`Confidence` (agent-derived) if both must appear in a row — a subtle style difference (e.g. outline/ghost badge for source-asserted vs filled badge for AI-derived) reduces the risk analysts conflate 'what the sensor said' with 'what the agent concluded' while scanning quickly, consistent with the badge semantics already in badges.tsx (`variant='outline'` is already used elsewhere as a neutral/undetermined visual).

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Shipping every possible column visible by default and relying on end-users to manually hide/reorder via a Columns menu — NN Group and Pencil & Paper both stress the product team must curate the default; a menu is a customization escape hatch, not a substitute for good defaults. The current Cases table ships all 17 columns visible on first load.
- Zebra striping combined with hover + selected + disabled row states — produces 5 competing shades of grey and actually hurts scannability versus a plain hairline divider + hover highlight.
- Color-only status/severity signaling, or red/green severity pairing — both are common colorblind-accessibility failures; require an icon + text alongside color, and prefer blue/orange contrasts over red/green when possible.
- Hardcoding a sticky table header's offset to `top: 0` in an app that already has its own persistent sticky top nav — the table header will render UNDER the app chrome (z-order) rather than just beneath it, so users lose the header exactly when they need it (mid-scroll).
- CSS-transform-based row virtualization used together with a real `<table>` element and a sticky header — transforms fight native table layout math and the sticky header silently breaks; use spacer rows/cells instead if virtualizing an actual `<table>`.
- Encoding too many overlapping 'how-bad/how-sure' axes as separate always-visible badge columns (severity, AI-severity, risk, confidence, verdict, disposition, urgency all at once) — this is a real anti-pattern the current Cases table already exhibits; best-in-class SIEMs (Defender XDR, Datadog) collapse these into one dominant score + reveal factors on click, not seven parallel badges racing for attention in one row.
- Multi-line, wrapped table cells for anything other than one explicitly-designated 'description'-style column — wrapping destroys the constant row height that makes a dense table fast to scan top-to-bottom; truncate + ellipsis + tooltip/hover-card instead.
- Building a reusable primitive capability (like this codebase's `rowAccent` severity band or `TableHeader`'s `sticky` prop) and then never actually invoking it from any real page — verify newly-designed density/scannability features are actually WIRED into the pages analysts use, not just available-but-unused in the component API.

**Sources:**

- https://www.nngroup.com/articles/data-tables/

- https://www.nngroup.com/videos/big-tables-small-screens/
