## Number/count-up animation UX for the TLSOC Security Command Center dashboard — when animated counters help vs. distract, timing/easing, and reduced-motion accessibility


**Key findings:**
- There is no dedicated academic literature on 'count-up counters' specifically, but the converging expert consensus (Nielsen Norman Group, Smashing Magazine) is that animation on numbers is justified ONLY to signal a STATE CHANGE / combat change-blindness, not as decoration. NN/g: 'Animations are less critical for UX when they are simply time-filling visual stimulations' and frequent animations must get shorter/more subtle the more often a user encounters them. Smashing Magazine's 2025 real-time-dashboard piece explicitly recommends fade-ins or count-up transitions specifically to counter 'change blindness — our tendency to miss non-salient changes' when a number updates live.
- Two DIFFERENT count-up patterns get conflated in the wild, with opposite risk profiles: (1) a one-shot 'reveal' count from 0→N on first mount/first paint (low risk, orienting, common on marketing/landing stat blocks — CountUp.js/Magic UI's NumberTicker default to a 2-4s cinematic reveal with easeInExpo/easeOutExpo), vs (2) a 'live re-tween' from old→new value while the tile is already on-screen and being actively read (higher risk — an analyst glancing mid-tween can misread an in-flight number as the settled value). SOC dashboards need pattern (1) restrained and pattern (2) used sparingly, fast, and only for meaningful deltas.
- Concrete timing consensus across sources: NN/g's 'Executing UX Animations' article gives 100-400ms as the general sweet spot for UI feedback (500ms+ 'feels burdensome'); Smashing's real-time-dashboard piece gives a MORE SPECIFIC band for numeric value updates: 200-400ms, vs. 100-150ms for direct user-action feedback and 300-600ms for chart-trend animation. This is much shorter than the 2-4s 'cinematic' count-up convention used by marketing counter libraries (CountUp.js/Odometer.js/Magic UI), which is the wrong reference class for an operational console.
- Easing consensus: ease-out (fast start, settle at the end) is the standard for anything appearing/entering/counting UP, because it reads as responsive while still letting the eye register the final resting value; linear motion 'looks weird and unnatural' (NN/g). Material Design 3 and Carbon (IBM's enterprise/'productive' motion mode) both formalize this as a small token set (standard/entrance/exit/emphasized curves) rather than bespoke per-component easing — Carbon explicitly frames 'productive' (fast, subtle, efficiency-signaling) motion as the correct mode for enterprise/ops software, as opposed to 'expressive' (slower, more visible) motion for consumer/marketing surfaces.
- Accessibility: `prefers-reduced-motion` (MDN, web.dev) is the mechanism, targeting vestibular disorders, migraines, seizure risk, and cognitive overload from non-essential motion. WCAG 2.3.1 (flash/seizure) and 2.3.3 (motion from interactions, AAA) are both narrower than 'any animated dashboard number' — 2.3.3 only covers animation TRIGGERED BY USER INTERACTION, so an auto-updating/data-driven count-up on a polling refresh isn't technically covered by a WCAG success criterion, but every accessibility source surveyed (web.dev's motion module, Pope Tech, Sarasoueidan) treats `prefers-reduced-motion` as the correct universal signal to honor regardless of trigger source.
- aria-live guidance from ARIA authoring practice (MDN's timer role) is directly relevant: a numeric ticking display has an IMPLIED `aria-live="off"` specifically to prevent every intermediate frame from being announced to screen-reader users — the pattern is to announce only a deliberate, coalesced, final value at intervals, never per-frame.
- Codebase-specific finding (from reading this repo, not the open web): the project's own global reduced-motion safety net in `webui/src/styles/theme.css` (lines 374-383) is a CSS-only reset — `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { * { animation-duration: 0.001ms !important; transition-duration: 0.001ms !important } }`. This neutralizes CSS `@keyframes`/`transition` animations (e.g. the existing `Stagger`/`animate-rise-in` reveal) but does NOT and CANNOT neutralize a JavaScript `requestAnimationFrame`/`setInterval`-driven number tween — that motion would keep running for reduced-motion users unless the count-up implementation separately checks the existing `usePrefersReducedMotion()` hook (`webui/src/soc/hooks/usePrefersReducedMotion.ts`) and skips straight to the target value.
- Codebase-specific finding: `KpiTile` (`webui/src/soc/components/KpiTile.tsx`) already renders its big value with `tabular-nums` (line 202) — the one CSS detail every count-up guide agrees is mandatory (fixed-width digit glyphs so the number doesn't visually jitter/reflow as digits change during the tween). This is already correct and should be preserved/reused, not reinvented.
- Codebase-specific finding: custom dashboards already auto-refresh on a `DASHBOARD_AUTO_REFRESH_MS = 60_000` cadence with visibility-awareness (`webui/src/soc/dashboard/DashboardDataProvider.tsx`, pauses via `document.hidden`), and `NotificationBell.tsx`/`TimeRangePicker.tsx` use the identical `document.hidden`-gated polling pattern elsewhere. This is exactly the scenario the research warns about: re-triggering a full count-up animation on every 60s background poll while an analyst is staring at the tile is the textbook 'distracting, gratuitous' case NN/g calls out — the animation must fire on a real value CHANGE the user hasn't already seen, not on every refresh tick, and should skip/snap when the tab is hidden.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Build count-up as a tiny, dependency-free `useCountUp(target: number, opts)` hook (≈30-40 lines, rAF-driven with `1 - Math.pow(1 - t, 3)` ease-out or reusing the existing `--motion-ease-standard: cubic-bezier(0.2,0,0,1)` curve evaluated in JS) rather than pulling in react-countup/CountUp.js/Odometer.js/framer-motion — CLAUDE.md's Round 4/5/6 baselines are all 'ZERO new runtime deps'; adding one for a single-number tween would break that invariant for no real benefit.
- Duration: default to 400-600ms (map to/near the existing `--motion-slow: 280ms` token, extended slightly for a count vs. a simple entrance), NOT the 2-4s 'cinematic' convention used by CountUp.js/Magic UI's NumberTicker — those libraries are tuned for landing-page hero stats meant to be watched once, which is the wrong reference class for a KPI a SOC analyst re-reads dozens of times per shift.
- Trigger rule (most important product decision): animate ONLY (a) on the tile's first mount in the session, and (b) on subsequent real value changes where `newValue !== previousValue` (diff via `useRef`, not on every re-render/poll). Explicitly do NOT restart the tween on a 60s `DASHBOARD_AUTO_REFRESH_MS` poll that returns the same number, and skip/snap-instant when `document.hidden` is true (reuse the exact pattern already in `DashboardDataProvider.tsx`/`NotificationBell.tsx`) so a background tab doesn't queue up a stale replay when the analyst tabs back.
- Gate every tween through the existing `usePrefersReducedMotion()` hook (`webui/src/soc/hooks/usePrefersReducedMotion.ts`): when true, set the DOM text to the target value in a single frame (duration = 0), no rAF loop at all. Do not rely on the global CSS reduced-motion reset in `theme.css` — verify explicitly, since that reset only zeroes CSS `animation-duration`/`transition-duration` and is blind to a JS-driven counter.
- Keep `tabular-nums` (already present in `KpiTile.tsx` line 202) and additionally reserve a stable min-width for the value slot (e.g. `min-width: Nch` sized to the max expected digit count, or a fixed grid column) so a growing digit count mid-tween (9 → 99 → 999) doesn't reflow the sibling delta-chip/icon — a commonly-missed detail in DIY count-up implementations.
- Run every intermediate frame value through the SAME formatter already used for the settled value (`fmtNumber`/`fmtMoney`/`fmtTokens` in `webui/src/lib/format.ts`), not a raw `Math.round(n)` — otherwise thousands separators/currency symbols visibly appear/disappear mid-count (e.g. '12345' then snapping to '12,345'), which reads as broken rather than polished.
- Scope count-up to clean INTEGER 'things we counted' tiles only — open-case count, alert count, entity count — and explicitly EXCLUDE money (`fmtMoney`, 2-4 decimal places) and percentage tiles from tweening; a flickering cents digit reads as noisy/glitchy, not trustworthy, whereas a snapping-into-place cost figure is fine. This also matches the qualitative distinction in the research between 'counting real, discrete things' (feels purposeful) and animating a computed/derived metric (feels gratuitous).
- For the KPI strip on Overview/Dashboards, compose the count-up start delay with the EXISTING `Stagger` component's per-item delay (`webui/src/soc/components/Stagger.tsx`, `step=60ms`) so the entrance fade-in and the number count read as one choreographed reveal, instead of 6-8 tiles all counting up in unison (which the research on multi-object animation timing flags as busy) or fighting the Stagger's own fade timing.
- Cap the surface where count-up applies to the small, fixed KPI-tile strip (`KpiTile`) — never apply it to a dense table/list column (case list counts, per-row metrics). Every source (NN/g, Smashing, Carbon's 'productive vs expressive' distinction) converges on: animation should be rare and reserved for the few headline numbers, not applied densely across a data grid.
- Wire the accessible announcement through the EXISTING `useLiveAnnouncer()` hook (`webui/src/soc/hooks/useLiveAnnouncer.ts`), calling `announce()` exactly ONCE with the settled final value/delta after the tween completes (e.g. 'Open cases: 42, up 3') — never per animation frame. This mirrors the ARIA `timer` role's implied `aria-live="off"` pattern and avoids screen-reader spam.
- For a large jump (e.g. an alert-count spike from 12 to 340 after a burst), do not run the fixed-duration tween through hundreds of increments — either snap directly with a brief highlight/pulse treatment (reusing the existing delta-arrow/color system already in `KpiTile`'s `resolveDelta()`), or cap the animation's step size so a big delta finishes in the same 400-600ms window rather than a comprehension-delaying multi-second crawl.
- Ship as a strictly opt-in prop path on `KpiTile` (e.g. an optional numeric `value` + `format` pairing that internally uses the hook) so the ~dozens of existing `KpiTile` call sites passing pre-formatted `React.ReactNode` strings are entirely unaffected — matches the codebase's stated 'additive, zero regressions' discipline (CLAUDE.md non-negotiable practice across every round).

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Applying the marketing/landing-page count-up convention (2-4s, `easeInExpo`, meant to be watched once) to an operational tile that gets glanced at dozens of times a shift — wrong reference class; ops motion should be Carbon's 'productive' (fast, subtle) mode, not 'expressive'.
- Assuming the project's existing global CSS `prefers-reduced-motion` reset (`theme.css` lines 374-383) automatically covers a JS-driven counter — it only zeroes CSS animation/transition durations and will silently keep tweening for reduced-motion users unless the hook explicitly checks `usePrefersReducedMotion()`.
- Re-triggering the full count-up on every background data poll (the dashboard's 60s `DASHBOARD_AUTO_REFRESH_MS` cadence) instead of only on a genuine value change — this is precisely the 'gratuitous/distracting' pattern NN/g and Smashing warn against, and on a security console it risks training analysts to tune the animation out (the same 'alert fatigue' dynamic SOC tooling already worries about for alerts, applied to motion).
- Wiring `aria-live` to update on every animation frame — spams screen-reader users with dozens of intermediate values per second instead of one coalesced final announcement (ARIA `timer` role explicitly defaults to `aria-live="off"` for this exact reason).
- Animating money/percentage tiles the same as integer counts — flickering decimal places read as glitchy/untrustworthy rather than polished, which matters acutely for a product whose whole pitch is trustworthy, auditable numbers.
- Pulling in a counter animation npm package (react-countup, Odometer.js, Magic UI's NumberTicker/Framer Motion) for what is a ~30-line rAF loop — violates this project's explicit zero-new-runtime-dependency discipline for no real gain.
- Not diffing against the previous value before animating — re-running the 0→N (or old→new) tween on every unrelated parent re-render (e.g. a filter keystroke, an unrelated store update) rather than only when the displayed number itself actually changed.
- Letting a large sudden delta (an alert-count spike) animate through hundreds of intermediate values at a fixed per-step timing, which can make an urgent number take multiple seconds to become legible right when speed of comprehension matters most — cap total duration regardless of delta size, or use a snap+pulse instead of a full count for big jumps.
- Losing thousands-separator/currency formatting mid-tween by animating a raw number instead of running the same formatter (`fmtNumber`/`fmtMoney`) used for the settled value on every intermediate frame.
- Applying count-up broadly across a dense table/list instead of reserving it for the small, fixed KPI-tile strip — every source converges on 'rare and reserved for headline numbers,' not applied per-row.

**Sources:**

## Micro-interactions & motion in analytics dashboards for the TLSOC Round-7 UI overhaul — Command Center dashboard, Noise Reduction funnel, and Case Story view


**Key findings:**
- Cross-industry consensus (Material Design, NN/g, Rauno Freiberg, Emil Kowalski) is that motion's job in a data product is feedback/state-signaling, never 'delight': NN/g frames motion as 'subtle feedback... rather than to induce delight or entertain users'; Material Design's 3 pillars (informative/focused/expressive) map to hierarchy/feedback/status, not decoration.
- Tight numeric consensus on duration: interactive feedback (hover/press/tooltip) should land under ~200ms (Rauno Freiberg: 'not more than 200ms... to feel immediate'; Emil Kowalski: tooltips ~125ms, dropdowns ~300ms, selects ~180ms 'feels more responsive than 400ms'), hard ceiling ~300ms for ordinary UI motion; only 'earned' reveals (dialogs, page entrances, milestone counters) run 400-600ms.
- The near-universal 'premium' easing curve for entrances is expo-out cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1) — ease-out only (fast start, decelerate). ease-in is explicitly wrong for appearing elements because it speeds up at the end and feels sluggish. Material's alternate default is cubic-bezier(0.4, 0, 0.2, 1). Our own webui/tailwind.config already standardizes on cubic-bezier(0.16,1,0.3,1) for fade-in/rise-in/hero-in/settings-highlight — i.e. the codebase already picked the industry-favorite curve, it's just not yet named/reused as a token.
- Only `transform` and `opacity` should be animated (GPU-composited, no layout thrash); box-shadow/height/width are reserved for cases with no transform equivalent (e.g. accordion height).
- Disney's 'follow-through and overlapping action' principle (via Rauno Freiberg's Web Interface Guidelines) — secondary/child elements trail the primary trigger by ~100-200ms rather than animating in perfect sync — is what makes multi-element reveals feel organic instead of a robotic simultaneous 'pop.' Our existing `Stagger.tsx` (60ms/item step, 600ms cap) already implements exactly this.
- Skeleton screens are perceived as 30-50% faster than identical-duration spinner loads (widely cited Facebook/LinkedIn-style findings) because they pre-communicate layout structure before content arrives — the brain starts processing 'the page is already here' rather than 'wait.'
- Reduced motion must be architectural, not per-component: the best-practice pattern is one global `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` CSS reset that zeroes all `animation-duration`/`transition-duration`, PLUS a JS hook (matchMedia-based) for anything imperative (JS tweens, D3/canvas transitions, one-shot inline style computations) that the CSS reset cannot reach. WCAG 2.3.3 (AAA) formalizes that any interaction-triggered animation must be disableable unless essential to the content.
- Chart-library entrance animations (Recharts/D3/Chart.js) are the single riskiest surface for 'gimmicky': naive `isAnimationActive`/default D3 transitions replay the ENTIRE draw-in on every prop update — i.e. on every polling refresh, not just first mount — which reads as flicker/jank in a monitoring tool and actively erodes trust (numbers/bars appear to 'reset and regrow' every 15-30s). Recharts GitHub issues (#1337, #945, #1310) confirm this is a known, unresolved rough edge, and that 'auto' reduced-motion detection is unreliable across versions.
- SOC-specific alert-fatigue research (ACM Computing Surveys 2026; Anomali/Swimlane/CyberDefenders industry write-ups) finds analysts already ignore ~62% of alerts and miss ~28% of critical ones under cognitive overload — the design implication is that decorative motion in a SOC UI has a real attentional cost and must be budgeted like a scarce resource, reserved for the one thing that genuinely needs the analyst's eye, never simultaneous multi-element flourishes.
- Toast/notification best practice explicitly discourages timed auto-dismiss animations for anything actionable or critical — reserve decaying-toast motion for save/ack confirmations; critical/escalation-worthy events need a persistent, non-timed affordance instead.
- KPI count-up vs. mechanical 'odometer' roll is a deliberate choice in the animated-number literature: continuous count-up suits routine, frequently-refreshing metrics; the odometer's individual-digit-lock is for genuinely rare 'milestone' moments. A SOC console's KPIs refresh often, so count-up (animating only the delta between old and new value, never 0→N) is the correct default.
- Linear's own account of their design refresh crystallizes what 'premium' actually means in a professional dashboard: 'If most people don't immediately notice what changed, that's probably a good sign' — premium reads as an invisible, coherent system (one easing curve, one duration scale, applied everywhere), achieved by removing flourishes, not adding them.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Formalize the easing/duration vocabulary implicit in webui/tailwind.config.js into named theme tokens (e.g. `transitionTimingFunction: { premium: 'cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)' }`) so every new Round-7 surface (Command Center, funnel, case story) writes `ease-premium` instead of re-typing the bezier or falling back to Tailwind's default ease-in-out. Document a small duration scale next to it in a comment (100/150ms press-hover, 250ms panel entrance, 400-600ms chart/gauge draw-in) matching the ceilings Kowalski/Rauno converge on.
- KpiTile.tsx: animate the numeric value by tweening from the PREVIOUS value to the NEW value (never 0→N) over ~400-500ms with `ease-premium`, via a small dependency-free `useAnimatedNumber(value)` hook (requestAnimationFrame, no new npm dep) rendered through the existing `tabular-nums` class. On first mount, render the number statically with no animation (avoid a '0→47' flash on page load) — mirroring the 'static on true mount, animate on true change' rule the chart libraries currently get wrong.
- Give clickable KpiTile a genuine but SOC-scaled 'lift': add `transition-transform duration-150 ease-premium hover:-translate-y-0.5` (2px, not the marketing-site -8px) alongside the existing `hover:border-primary/40 hover:bg-accent/30`, paired with a one-step rise to the already-defined `shadow-elev1` token — reuses the existing elevation vocabulary instead of inventing new shadow values.
- Fix charts.tsx's blanket `isAnimationActive={false}`: switch to mount-only draw-in. Track `hasAnimatedRef` per chart instance, keyed so the chart only remounts (and thus re-plays) when the operator changes the time-range/filter (`key={metricsRangeKey}`), never on the background poll. Pass `isAnimationActive={!hasAnimatedRef.current}`, flip the ref in `onAnimationEnd`, duration 400-600ms with `ease-premium`. When `usePrefersReducedMotion()` is true, force `isAnimationActive={false}` unconditionally — do not rely on recharts' own 'auto' reduced-motion detection (documented as inconsistent across versions).
- Reuse RiskGauge.tsx's existing pattern — a plain CSS `transition-[stroke-dashoffset] duration-500` fired only when the underlying score prop changes — as the reference technique for any new gauge-like widget in the Command Center (posture ring, MTTA/MTTR burn-down). No framer-motion, no new library; this is already the right pattern in the codebase, just needs to be the DELIBERATE template for new widgets rather than a one-off.
- Live/streaming indicator: reuse the already-defined `pulse` Tailwind keyframe (opacity 1↔0.55) for the SSE 'connected' dot. One such dot per screen, ≤8px, colored `bg-success`/`bg-info` — never a severity color — so a pulsing red dot next to an open-case count doesn't read as an alarm.
- New-item arrival (a case/alert lands via SSE while the list is open): reuse the existing `settings-highlight` keyframe (box-shadow ring pulse only, zero transform, ~1.4-1.6s, already reduced-motion-safe by construction) as the 'new row' cue, or the existing `rise-in` for prepend-to-top — pick one, don't combine both, and keep the highlight ring on the neutral `--ring` token so the badge's own severity color (not the entrance animation) carries the urgency signal.
- Noise Reduction funnel infographic: build it as ONE inline SVG with each stage/edge as a `<path>` whose `stroke-dasharray`/`stroke-dashoffset` transitions on mount, staggered stage-by-stage left→right at ~120-150ms offsets per stage — reuse `Stagger.tsx`'s existing delay-step mechanism verbatim rather than a bespoke implementation, mirroring the funnel's real causal order (alerts → correlated → auto-cleared / escalated).
- Sync each funnel stage's count-up to that stage's own reveal delay (not all four numbers ticking simultaneously) — sequencing, not simultaneity, is what makes it read as a narrated pipeline rather than four charts that happened to load together.
- Gate the funnel's entrance animation on query identity: wrap it in a component keyed by the effective time-range/filter params so React only remounts (and replays the draw-in) when the analyst changes the window; a background refresh of the SAME window should only patch the numbers via the count-up tween, never redraw the paths.
- Explicitly verify `usePrefersReducedMotion()` for the funnel's SVG stroke-offset logic: the project's blanket CSS reset in theme.css (lines ~373-383) only zeroes `transition-duration`/`animation-duration`, it does NOT stop a one-shot JS-computed initial dash-offset value — this is a real gap for any inline-style/JS-driven entrance and needs an explicit branch, same pattern already used for the Settings deep-link highlight.
- Case story view: use motion as an epistemic signal per NN/g's 'motion communicates state' principle. Render the SIEM-asserted facts panel with NO bespoke entrance motion (page-level `fade-in` at most) since facts are static ground truth being displayed, not generated. Render the AI-derived assessment/verdict panel with the existing `rise-in` (fade+8px, 240ms, ease-premium) fired once per case load — this visually reinforces 'this was composed for you' without a typewriter/streaming-text effect (reserve char-by-char streaming for the live chat surface where tokens genuinely arrive over time; the case verdict is already fully computed server-side, so simulating typing is gimmicky, not honest).
- Reuse `Stagger.tsx` verbatim for both the evidence/fact list AND the ReAct steps in `TraceTimeline.tsx` (its default 60ms step / 600ms cap already sits inside the duration ceilings from the research) instead of building a bespoke stagger for the trace timeline — one shared primitive keeps the whole app's motion feeling like one system.
- Gate the case-open reveal (TraceTimeline / evidence Stagger) on `key={case.id}` so switching between two cases replays the reveal once, but a background poll re-rendering the SAME open case never re-triggers it — same 'mount identity vs. data identity' rule as the charts fix.
- Never apply spring/overshoot easing to severity, verdict, or disposition badges — a bounce reads as playful and undercuts the seriousness the color/icon/SEMANTIC_ICON is already signaling. Use flat ease-premium fade/rise or no motion at all for badges appearing inside already-rendered static content.
- Close/escalate disposition feedback should stay calm: a checkmark icon cross-fade + the row settling into its new status color — no celebratory pop/confetti/scale-bounce on closing a case as false positive, since adjacent rows may still be open/critical and a celebratory micro-interaction reads as tonally wrong next to unresolved risk.
- Add a `useAnimatedNumber`/chart-draw-in reduced-motion regression test alongside the existing pattern (`KpiTile.delta-neutral.test.tsx`, `RiskGauge.bands.test.tsx`) asserting the final value/state renders synchronously when `matchMedia` reports `reduce` — mirrors how the app already unit-tests its motion-adjacent components.
- Document the finalized easing/duration scale directly in theme.css next to the existing color-token rationale comments, matching the project's established documentation style, so the next agent working on Round 7 doesn't reinvent per-component timing.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Animation replaying on every poll/refresh (charts, funnel, KPI 0→N reset) instead of only on true mount/identity change — reads as flicker and actively erodes analyst trust in a monitoring tool ('did the number just glitch?').
- Decorative/infinite looping motion (parallax, 3D tilt, floating gradient blobs) on data-dense screens — acceptable on a marketing/login hero (the project already scopes `aurora-a/b` to the login page only) but wrong on the Command Center or case view.
- Bounce/spring overshoot on severity, verdict, or disposition elements — undermines the seriousness the color/icon is signaling; premium security tools read as controlled, not playful.
- Celebratory/confetti-style feedback on closing or auto-clearing a case — tonally wrong when other cases on screen remain open/critical; this is exactly the class of 'delight' motion NN/g flags as inappropriate for feedback-oriented interfaces.
- Timed auto-dismiss toasts for anything actionable or critical — reserve decaying-toast motion strictly for save/ack confirmations, per accessibility guidance and the fact that a swiped-away toast can hide something the analyst needed to act on.
- Relying on a chart library's built-in 'auto' reduced-motion detection instead of explicitly branching on the app's own `usePrefersReducedMotion()` hook — behavior is documented as inconsistent across recharts versions.
- Assuming the global CSS `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` reset is sufficient for JS-driven/imperative animations (D3 transitions, requestAnimationFrame tweens, one-shot inline stroke-dashoffset computed at mount) — it isn't; it only neutralizes CSS `transition`/`animation` properties, not JS-computed initial values.
- Motion as the SOLE carrier of meaning (e.g., only a moving highlight indicates 'this row is new,' with no other affordance) — violates both WCAG 2.3.3 and the project's own established non-color/non-motion-only signaling precedent (SEMANTIC_ICON, band labels).
- Per-component bespoke easing/duration (a new component inventing its own ease-in-out or a random 350ms) instead of reusing the established cubic-bezier(0.16,1,0.3,1) and the small duration scale already implicit in the codebase — breaks the 'one coherent system' feel that Linear, NN/g, and Material all identify as what makes motion read as premium rather than ad hoc.
- Adding a new animation/motion npm dependency (framer-motion, GSAP, react-spring) for any of this — the project already removed framer-motion in Round 5 for being an unused dependency; every recommendation above is achievable with existing Tailwind keyframes/transition utilities plus the already-built `Stagger`, `usePrefersReducedMotion`, and `settings-highlight` patterns.
- Over-scaling hover lift on dense, small elements (KPI tiles, table rows, badges) with a large -8px 'marketing card' translateY — wrong scale for a data-dense console; keep lifts to 1-2px and short duration, or skip entirely on anything smaller than a case-card.

## Color for severity/risk in security UIs — colorblind-safe palettes, WCAG contrast, non-color redundancy, avoiding red/green traps (informing the TLSOC Round-7 UI overhaul)


**Key findings:**
- WCAG tracks two DIFFERENT, non-interchangeable contrast bars for color: SC 1.4.3 text contrast (≥4.5:1 for normal text) and SC 1.4.11 non-text/graphical-object contrast (≥3:1 for icons, status swatches, and UI-component boundaries against their adjacent background) — plus SC 1.4.1 (Level A): color may never be the ONLY channel conveying meaning (error/status/required-field/data-viz all named as failure examples). Source: w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/non-text-contrast.html, w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/use-of-color.html, w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Techniques/general/G207.
- Every mature design system converges on the same triad for status/severity: color + shape/icon + text label, never color alone, because red/green is indistinguishable to CVD users. PatternFly's severity ladder (Critical/Important/Moderate/Minor/None/Undefined) assigns a DIFFERENT icon shape per level with escalating 'visual weight' as severity rises, and states outright: 'status colors and status icons should not be swapped or rearranged.' IBM Carbon's status-indicator pattern is built on the same 'color + shape + symbol' triad for exactly the same reason. Source: patternfly.org/patterns/status-and-severity/, carbondesignsystem.com/patterns/status-indicator-pattern/ (also v10.carbondesignsystem.com/patterns/status-indicator-pattern/).
- Okabe-Ito (aka the 'Wong' palette, the Nature Methods recommended standard) is the reference CVD-safe CATEGORICAL palette — 8 hues (orange #E69F00, sky-blue #56B4E9, bluish-green #009E73, yellow #F0E442, blue #0072B2, vermillion #D55E00, reddish-purple #CC79A7, black) — intended for IDENTITY-ARBITRARY series (e.g. per-model bars), never for severity semantics. Viridis is the reference perceptually-uniform SEQUENTIAL ramp: luminance increases monotonically, so it survives grayscale printing and remains legible under deuteranopia/protanopia — correct for heatmaps/continuous risk gradients, wrong for discrete severity chips (a gradient can't communicate 5 discrete named bands as reliably as hue+label can). Source: conceptviz.app/blog/okabe-ito-palette-hex-codes-complete-reference, cran.r-project.org/web/packages/viridis/vignettes/intro-to-viridis.html, journal.r-project.org/articles/RJ-2023-071/.
- Red-green is THE classic trap: ~8% of men have red-green CVD (protanopia/deuteranopia), and red/orange/yellow/green all sit on the same 'confusion axis,' collapsing toward indistinguishable brown/tan. Best-in-class fix used across the industry: replace green with blue/teal at the 'safe/low' end of a severity ladder, and keep red only at the 'danger' end paired with an icon/label — 'reds and oranges look the same to red-green colorblind people... use an orange border around text for a medium alert vs. red background/white text for high.' Source: medium.com/@courtneyjordan/designing-color-blind-accessible-dashboards-ba3e0084be82, tableau.com/blog/examining-data-viz-rules-dont-use-red-green-together, baselinehq.com/blog/colourblindness-information-ui-design-red-green-problems-tips-tricks.html.
- Industry severity-scoring systems converge on near-identical numeric band cut points and colors: CVSS None/Low/Medium/High/Critical maps to gray→green/blue→orange/yellow→red→dark-red; Elastic Security's published risk-score bands are Low 0-21 / Medium 22-47 / High 48-73 / Critical 74-100. This EXACTLY matches the SCORE_BANDS ladder already hardcoded in our own webui/src/soc/components/palette.ts (scoreBand()) — confirming that ladder is industry-aligned, not arbitrary. Source: first.org/cvss/specification-document, elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/advanced-entity-analytics/view-analyze-risk-score-data, webasha.com/blog/understanding-cvss-severity-levels-and-ratings-complete-guide.
- Dark-mode severity colors need INDEPENDENT re-tuning, not a direct reuse of light-mode hues: highly saturated colors vibrate/cause eye strain against dark backgrounds; best practice desaturates ~20-30% and raises LIGHTNESS (not saturation) for dark surfaces, and every token needs its OWN measured contrast ratio per theme (a token that clears AA in light mode can fail in dark mode or vice versa). Source: uxcel.com/blog/12-principles-of-dark-mode-design-627, devpalettes.com/blog/dark-mode-color-guide/.
- CVD-safety is treated as an AUTOMATABLE CI gate by accessibility-mature engineering orgs — using the standard Machado/Viénot dichromacy simulation matrices plus a perceptual delta (CIE ΔE) threshold in axe-core/Pa11y/Chrome-DevTools-style pipelines — not a one-time manual 'ran it through Coblis' check. Source: colorblind.io/guides/testing-website-accessibility, color-blindness.com/coblis-color-blindness-simulator/.
- 'Traffic-light overload' is a documented anti-pattern: washing many simultaneous rows/tiles in saturated red/amber/green causes information overload and defeats prioritization ('viewing 50 pieces of information at once... users feel stressed, find it hard to identify important information'); the fix is to reserve full-saturation color for a SINGLE focal indicator (one badge/gauge), not entire rows across a dense list. Source: bernardmarr.com/performance-reporting-how-to-use-traffic-light-colours-and-rag-ratings-in-dashboards/, staceybarr.com/measure-up/3-problems-with-traditional-kpi-traffic-lights/.
- Emerging 2025-2026 UX convention for AI-derived vs human/system-asserted content: a distinct, explicit visual marker (small badge/icon/confidence affordance) beside AI-attributed judgments, separate from the base content's own color-coding, so provenance is never inferred from hue alone — directly relevant to separating 'SIEM-asserted severity' from 'AI-derived verdict' in a case story view. Source: koruux.com/ai-patterns-for-ui-design/, groovyweb.co/blog/ui-ux-design-trends-ai-apps-2026.
- CODEBASE-SPECIFIC (verified by reading webui/src/soc/components/palette.ts + webui/src/styles/theme.css): our product already implements most of this textbook — 3 orthogonal semantic axes (SEVERITY/STATUS/VERDICT) each carrying a fill + on-fill-foreground + standalone-text triple; SEVERITY has NO green (low=blue); green is reserved solely for STATUS (resolved/closed) and always paired with a Check icon; every badge renders a SEMANTIC_ICON glyph beside color (WCAG 1.4.1); an Okabe-Ito --chart-1..8 ramp exists for arbitrary series; a viridis sequential() ramp exists for heatmaps; two automated CI gates already exist (webui/scripts/gate-contrast.mjs, webui/scripts/gate-cvd.mjs) that measure real WCAG contrast and CVD-collapse (ΔE*ab under Machado/Viénot protan/deutan/tritan matrices) on every commit. This is a strong baseline for Round 7 to extend, not rebuild.
- VERIFIED GAP #1 (computed with the WCAG relative-luminance formula against the actual HSL values in theme.css): the light-theme --medium severity fill (gold, HSL 40 96% 40%) measures 2.97:1 against --card (white) and 2.89:1 against --canvas — BELOW the WCAG 1.4.11 3:1 non-text/graphical-object minimum. gate-contrast.mjs's NON_TEXT_AXES list checks --ring/--border-strong/--input/--chart-1..8 against --card but does NOT include --critical/--high/--medium/--low/--info/--success/--warning as graphical objects, so this out-of-spec fill currently passes CI. Any future use of the raw 'medium' fill as an unlabelled swatch/dot/left-border-accent/icon-tint (rather than the already-compliant --medium-text) would fail 1.4.11 for low-vision users in light mode. (Dark-theme --medium is fine at 9.57:1; light-theme --high/--critical/--low/--info all clear 3:1 comfortably — --medium in light mode is the one outlier.)
- VERIFIED GAP #2 (independently re-ran the same ΔE*ab / Machado-Viénot dichromacy method gate-cvd.mjs uses, against SEVERITY_COLOR's actual HSL values): gate-cvd.mjs's CVD-collapse regression test currently ONLY covers the 8 identity-arbitrary --chart-* tokens — it has no equivalent test for SEVERITY_COLOR/STATUS_COLOR/VERDICT_COLOR, which is the highest-stakes axis in the entire product (a collapsed critical-vs-high reading under CVD would misdirect triage attention). Today's actual severity tokens DO clear the JND floor when I simulate them (closest pair: high vs medium under deuteranopia, ΔE≈7.6, well above the 2.3 JND floor and even above the 5.0 stricter bar I'd recommend) — so there is no live bug, but there is a live regression risk: nothing in CI stops a future hue nudge to --high or --medium from silently collapsing this axis under CVD, because the safety net doesn't cover it.
- VERIFIED design implication (computed): pairwise fill-vs-fill contrast between adjacent severity swatches is inherently very low by design (critical vs high ≈1.40:1, low vs info ≈1.02:1, high vs medium ≈1.34:1) — a 5-band hue ladder cannot also be a 5-band luminance ladder. This is expected and is EXACTLY why the icon/label redundancy channel matters, but it also means any stacked-bar/heatmap/sparkline visualization that lets two severity fills touch directly (no stroke/gap) will look like one blurred gradient rather than distinct bands, for ALL users, not just CVD ones.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Extend webui/scripts/gate-contrast.mjs's NON_TEXT_AXES array to add {name:'critical on card', fg:'--critical', bg:'--card'} and the same for high/medium/low/info/success/warning (mirroring the existing chart-1..8 entries), so any raw semantic fill used as a graphical object is held to the same ≥3:1 WCAG 1.4.11 bar. This will immediately fail light-theme --medium (2.97:1 measured) — fix by nudging --medium's lightness down a few points (e.g. L 40%→36-37%) or raising saturation slightly against white, then re-run the gate and re-check the dependent --medium-text/--medium-foreground pairs still clear their own bars.
- Extend webui/scripts/gate-cvd.mjs to run its existing ΔE*ab / Machado-Viénot dichromacy simulation against SEVERITY_COLOR, STATUS_COLOR, and VERDICT_COLOR token values (not just --chart-*), and make this a permanent CI regression gate rather than a one-off manual pass like the one done for this research task. Given the stakes (misreading critical-vs-high), use a stricter floor than the chart ramp's 2.3 JND — recommend ≥5.0 ΔE*ab for this axis specifically.
- Never let two severity/status/verdict fills abut directly with no separator: wherever these swatches sit adjacent (stacked bars, MITRE heatmap cells, mini legends, sparkline dots), render a 1-2px stroke or gap in --card/--border color between segments — pairwise fill contrast between bands is as low as ~1.0-1.4:1 by design, so a shared edge alone will not read as a boundary for anyone, CVD or not.
- In dense list/table views (cases table, alert queue), keep row backgrounds neutral (--card) and let the SEVERITY/VERDICT color live ONLY in the per-row badge/chip — do not wash whole rows in severity color across many simultaneous rows, which reproduces the well-documented 'traffic-light overload' failure mode from RAG-status dashboards. Audit DataTable/cases-list row styling in Round 7 to confirm this holds (full-row color washing should be reserved, at most, for a single focal alert banner, not a repeated list).
- For the case 'story' view's SIEM-fact vs AI-assessment split specifically: add one more non-color differentiator on the VERDICT axis alone (not severity) — e.g. a small distinct glyph or an explicit 'AI verdict' microlabel beside the verdict chip — so provenance is never inferred from hue alone. Today VERDICT_COLOR reuses the SAME hue tokens as SEVERITY_COLOR (e.g. true_positive→critical) and only SEMANTIC_ICON differentiates them (CircleDot vs AlertOctagon); that clears WCAG 1.4.1 but a labeled provenance affordance removes residual ambiguity in exactly the view this task is meant to inform.
- Keep documenting measured ratios as inline comments in theme.css (e.g. '/* 6.36:1 */') but treat gate-contrast.mjs/gate-cvd.mjs as the SOLE source of truth going forward — wire every new severity-adjacent token through both gates before merge; a stale hand-written comment that looks verified but isn't is worse than no comment.
- Add a one-line pre-merge checklist item for Round 7 (alongside the existing npm run build / vitest / lint gates): any new severity/status/verdict hue must pass gate-contrast.mjs + the extended gate-cvd.mjs AND be spot-checked in Chrome DevTools 'Emulate vision deficiencies' (protanopia/deuteranopia/tritanopia/achromatopsia) before merge.
- Preserve, do not regress, the two things this codebase already gets right that are easy to accidentally break in a redesign: (a) SEVERITY axis has zero green (low=blue, not green) — keep green scoped exclusively to STATUS (resolved/closed), always paired with the Check icon; (b) every badge/gauge already shows a non-color icon or word label (SEMANTIC_ICON, RiskGauge's BAND_LABEL text) — any new severity-bearing component in Round 7 (new KPI tiles, campaign chips, baseline gauges) must plug into the SAME palette.ts/SEMANTIC_ICON authority rather than hardcoding a fresh color+no-icon treatment.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Relying on hue alone for adjacent same-shape swatches (stacked bars, heatmap cells, touching legend chips) — pairwise WCAG contrast between severity hues in this palette is as low as ~1.0-1.4:1, effectively invisible without a stroke or label between them.
- Using a raw semantic fill token (--critical/--high/--medium/...) directly as an icon/dot/border color without checking WCAG 1.4.11 separately from text contrast — only the -text variant was tuned/measured as standalone text; the base fill was tuned for on-fill TEXT contrast (badge label), not for use as a bare graphical object against the page background (this is precisely how --medium's 2.97:1 gap went unnoticed).
- Treating colorblind-safety as a one-time manual check ('we ran it through Coblis at launch') instead of an automated CI regression gate — palettes drift across multiple redesign rounds (this repo has already had 6), and a later hue nudge can silently reintroduce a CVD collapse with nothing to catch it, exactly the gap found in gate-cvd.mjs's chart-only coverage.
- Full-row or full-card saturated color washes for severity across a list of many items — reproduces the 'everything is red' traffic-light-fatigue effect that defeats the entire point of a triage queue; reserve full-saturation fills for one focal badge/gauge per row.
- Using green anywhere near a security 'positive/negative' ladder, even for an intuitive 'benign/false-positive = green' mapping — this both creates the classic red-green trap for ~8% of colorblind users AND semantically collides with green='resolved/success' on the STATUS axis, overloading one color with two different meanings in the same product.
- Mapping a raw severity NUMBER to a color band using scale-relative/magnitude inference instead of an explicit known scaleMax per source — this codebase's own comments note they already killed exactly this bug once (a raw 15 on a 0-15 Wazuh scale rendered Critical while a 16 on a different assumed scale rendered Low, a non-monotonic mapping); any new per-source severity number added in Round 7 must pass an explicit scaleMax, never inferred magnitude.
- Giving an AI-derived verdict the identical color AND no additional differentiator from a raw SIEM-derived severity score — analysts can misread 'the AI is highly confident this is malicious' as 'the underlying event itself is Critical severity' if the two axes are visually identical apart from a small icon swap, which is exactly the ambiguity the case-story view is meant to resolve.

**Sources:**

## KPI tile / stat-card design patterns (value + label + trend delta + sparkline) for a calm, scannable SOC command-center row


**Key findings:**
- Best-in-class anatomy (Stripe, Tremor's "KPI Card", Power BI 2025+ Card visual) is a fixed layer stack: small muted uppercase LABEL -> big tabular-figure VALUE -> DELTA (arrow + signed %, colored by judgment not raw sign) -> optional inline SPARKLINE -> a sub-line stating the comparison window/target. Source: nastengraph.substack.com/p/anatomy-of-the-kpi-card, tabulareditor.com/blog/kpi-card-best-practices-dashboard-design.
- Grafana/Datadog stat-panel convention: show exactly ONE value per tile (use "last", not an aggregate - "now" is the interesting point); color a small threshold chip/background, never fill the whole card ('your whole screen should look calm/green most of the time; eyes shouldn't have to hunt for color in tiny graph lines'); pack FEW tiles per row so the font stays large. Source: grafana.com/docs/grafana/latest/visualizations/panels-visualizations/visualizations/stat/.
- NN/g preattentive-processing guidance: position and length communicate magnitude fastest; color should reinforce a category/judgment, never be the ONLY channel; avoid donut/gauge/3D encodings on a scannable row. Source: nngroup.com/articles/dashboards-preattentive/.
- Cognitive-load research on KPI count: working memory tops out around 5-7 items; comprehension and decision speed drop sharply past 7-9 metrics on one view. Operational (daily) dashboards should target 5-7, not more; when you need more, the Power BI/Tremor convention is a 2-row grid of 4 wide tiles (wide enough to host a sparkline), not one cramped row of 7+. Source: datawirefra.me/blog/how-many-kpis-on-a-dashboard.
- WCAG 2.1 SC 1.4.1 (Use of Color) + accessibility practice: never encode good/bad by color alone on a delta - pair with an icon (arrow) AND an accessible text label (e.g. "changed up by 12%, worse"); screen readers don't announce color. Source: w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/use-of-color.html, accessibility.chat/articles/when-color-coding-fails-why-status-indicators-need-more-than-pretty-colors.
- Sparkline data-density rule: fewer than ~5 points collapses to a near-straight line that implies more precision/insight than exists; the useful range is roughly 7-30 points; scale must be PER-CARD (each sparkline auto-scales to its own min/max) - never a shared fixed y-axis across tiles of different magnitude, and never fabricate a flat line from 1-2 points. Source: datacamp.com/tutorial/excel-sparklines-complete-guide, support.microsoft.com/en-us/office/use-sparklines-to-show-data-trends.
- Every delta needs an explicit, honest comparison-window label ("vs previous 24h", "WoW") stated ONCE for the row rather than repeated per-tile chatter - ambiguity between window-scoped and all-time metrics sitting side by side erodes trust fastest.
- SOC-specific: the metrics that actually drive action on a triage lead's daily view are queue/backlog counts (open, critical/high, escalated-to-human) and QUALITY/noise metrics (false-positive rate, alert:incident ratio - industry benchmark targets FP rate <25% and alert:incident ~3:1 or better) - not inventory/vanity counts (corpus size, entity count) or cost, which belong in a secondary row/section. Source: underdefense.com/blog/soc-metrics/, secure.com/blog/soc/soc-metrics-and-kpis (0.5% signal-to-noise in an unmanaged SOC; 73% of teams cite false positives as their #1 detection challenge per the 2025 SANS D&R survey).
- In the OUR codebase specifically: webui/src/soc/components/KpiTile.tsx already implements the WCAG-correct pattern precisely - resolveDelta() splits the ARROW (always the true numeric direction) from the COLOR (goodDirection-driven judgment, success/critical/muted) and emits a single role="img" aria-label announcing both direction and judgement together. This is a real, previously-fixed bug (Round-5 'Bug #2', DESIGN_STANDARD Sec5.3: '+30% open alerts' must NOT render green just because the sign is positive). Keep this exact split when adding sparklines.
- In OUR codebase specifically: the backend (backend/app/engine/metrics.py, _compare_block/_delta_pct) and the GET /api/metrics/posture?compare=prev endpoint ALREADY compute period-over-period deltas for case_count, false_positive_rate, escalation_rate, automation_rate, alert_to_incident_ratio, mtta_p50, mttr_p50 (webui/src/soc/pages/Metrics.posture.api.ts PostureCompare/CompareBlock types), and Metrics.tsx already wires them into KpiTile's delta prop successfully (lines ~934-1006, via a deltaView() helper in posture.format.ts) - but Overview.tsx (the flagship 'Security Command Center' dashboard) calls usePosture(hours) with NO 'prev' argument (line 233), so posture.compare is always undefined there, and its local kpis[] type (lines 441-449) has no delta field at all - the KPI strip literally cannot show a trend delta today despite 100% of the plumbing already existing and proven elsewhere in the app.
- In OUR codebase specifically: a working, theme-aware, axis-less <Sparkline> component already exists (webui/src/soc/components/charts.tsx lines 424-476, used today only inside BaselineGauge.tsx for percentile trends) but KpiTile.tsx has NO sparkline slot/prop at all - so even where a KPI has real time-series data, there is no way to render it inline on the tile today.

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Wire the Overview KPI strip to the delta data that already exists end-to-end: change webui/src/soc/pages/Overview.tsx line 233 from usePosture(hours) to usePosture(hours, 'prev'); extend the local kpis[] object type (lines 441-449) with an optional delta?: { value: number; label?: string }; populate it from posture.compare.case_count / .escalation_rate / .false_positive_rate (already typed in Metrics.posture.api.ts's PostureCompare) using the same deltaView() mapping Metrics.tsx already uses; then pass delta={kpi.delta} in the .map() render at ~line 690-699. This is a same-shape reuse of existing, tested plumbing - zero new backend work, zero new components.
- Add a false-positive-rate / noise-ratio tile to the Overview strip. The single most action-driving SOC metric (quality.false_positive_rate, already computed server-side) currently only appears on the separate Metrics tab, not on the primary command-center view where a triage lead actually looks first.
- Trim the Overview KPI strip from 7 tiles to a primary row of 4-5 'signal' tiles per the 5-7-item cognitive ceiling and the Power BI/Tremor '4-wide, add a second row if needed' convention. Group the current 7 (Open Cases, Total Cases, Critical/High, Escalated To Human, Artifacts In Scope, Knowledge Signals, LLM Spend - Overview.tsx lines 451-533) by what a lead reacts to daily: keep Open Cases / Critical-High / Escalated-to-Human / (new) FP-rate as the primary row; demote Total Cases (all-time, no action), Artifacts In Scope, Knowledge Signals (RAG corpus size - an admin/system stat) and LLM Spend into the existing widget-grid DashboardGroup sections below (Cost already has its own CostBudgetWidget widget; Knowledge already has its own page) rather than mixing counts + a corpus size + a dollar figure in one flat row.
- Add an optional sparkline slot to KpiTile itself (webui/src/soc/components/KpiTile.tsx): add spark?: number[] (+ optional sparkAriaLabel), render the EXISTING <Sparkline data={spark} height={28} colorToken={accent} fill className="mt-2" /> from '@/soc/components/charts' under the value/delta row, gated strictly on spark && spark.length >= 5 so a fake near-straight line from a couple of points is never drawn - reuses an already-built, theme-consistent component (zero new deps), matching the project's 'ZERO new runtime deps' rule.
- Mark the sparkline aria-hidden/decorative - the accessible trend narrative should continue to live solely in the delta's role="img" aria-label (KpiTile.tsx resolveDelta()); don't let the spark become a second, potentially conflicting announcement channel for screen-reader users.
- Only wire spark data into tiles that have a REAL bucketed series today (or can cheaply get one) - start with Metrics.tsx's lifecycle tiles (MTTA/MTTR/dwell p50, which already have a percentile-bucket pattern precedented by BaselinePercentileSparkline in BaselineGauge.tsx) before Overview; leave tiles like 'Knowledge Signals' or 'LLM Spend' as value-only until a daily trend endpoint exists rather than inventing one.
- De-duplicate the delta-mapping helper: deltaView() currently lives only in webui/src/soc/pages/posture.format.ts and is imported by Metrics.tsx; when Overview.tsx needs the same CompareBlock -> KpiDelta mapping, import/reuse that same helper (or hoist it into a shared lib) rather than re-implementing it - small DRY win, zero behavior risk.
- State the comparison window ONCE per row, not per-tile: mirror Metrics.tsx's existing single caption ('Deltas compare the last {windowLabel} against the immediately-preceding equal window...', ~line 1012-1017) under Overview's KPI strip, reusing the windowLabel/TimeRangePicker state Overview.tsx already holds, instead of repeating '(vs prev 24h)' text on every tile.
- Preserve KpiTile's current restrained visual grammar when adding sparklines/deltas: hairline border, no resting shadow, a single soft-tinted icon chip (or thin left bar in variant='bar') as the ONLY accent color per tile - do not add a colored full-card background/threshold fill for routine metric movement; reserve a 'loud' full-tile treatment (if ever added) for genuine breach states (e.g. SLA-breached), matching the existing calm command-center aesthetic and the Grafana 'the screen should look calm most of the time' guidance.
- Keep (do not regress) two things this codebase already does correctly, validated against outside best practice: (1) KpiTile's arrow-shows-true-direction / color-shows-goodDirection-judgment split with a combined aria-label (the Round-5 'dead-field'/Bug#2 fix) - this is the textbook WCAG-1.4.1-compliant pattern; (2) goodDirection: 'none' on scope/inventory metrics like 'Total Cases' and 'Artifacts In Scope', which correctly withholds a judgment color where there is no better/worse.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Coloring a delta by the raw sign of the number instead of by whether the metric actually improved - '+30% open alerts' must render as a regression (critical), not green just because the sign is positive; the arrow must always show true direction, color must show judgment - these are two separate decisions.
- Relying on color alone to convey a trend's meaning (WCAG SC 1.4.1) - always pair color with an icon (arrow) and an accessible text label; screen readers announce neither hue nor a bare colored span.
- Drawing a sparkline from fewer than ~5 data points - a 2-3-point line looks precise but conveys nothing beyond 'up or down' and can be actively misleading; suppress the sparkline slot entirely rather than render a near-straight, over-confident segment.
- Sharing one fixed y-axis scale across sparklines on different tiles/magnitudes - each spark must auto-scale to its own series, or a small-magnitude metric will look artificially flat next to a large one.
- Cramming 7+ heterogeneous tiles into one flat KPI row 'because we have the data' - comprehension drops sharply past 7-9 items, and mixing raw counts, a corpus size, and a dollar figure in one row reads as 'a pile of stats' rather than 'here is what needs my attention today'.
- Placing an all-time metric (e.g. 'Total Cases') beside window-scoped deltas without explicitly saying so - ambiguous timeframes are one of the most common causes of KPI-row distrust; either scope everything to the same window or visibly label the exception.
- Adding a full-card colored background/threshold fill for routine, everyday metric fluctuation - reserve 'loud' full-tile alarming treatment for genuine breach conditions so the row stays calm/ambient rather than reading as constantly alarmed.
- Inventing a trend or sparkline for a metric with no real repeated-series data behind it yet (e.g. a corpus-size or cost tile with no daily history tracked) - ship it as a value-only tile instead of faking a series.
- Generating 'Smart Narrative' AI text under every KPI ('why this changed') as a default-on feature - it adds noise and a second cognitive load on a surface whose whole point is a half-second glance; if added at all, gate it behind an explicit expand/drill-in interaction, never inline by default.

**Sources:**
- https://nastengraph.substack.com/p/anatomy-of-the-kpi-card
- https://tabulareditor.com/blog/kpi-card-best-practices-dashboard-design
- https://www.nngroup.com/articles/dashboards-preattentive/
- https://grafana.com/docs/grafana/latest/visualizations/panels-visualizations/visualizations/stat/
- https://www.datawirefra.me/blog/how-many-kpis-on-a-dashboard
- https://www.datawirefra.me/blog/dashboard-layout-patterns
- https://www.tremor.so/

## Single risk-index visualization — gauge vs big-number vs radial; when a gauge helps; making one "risk score" instantly legible in a header


**Key findings:**
- Classic dataviz critique (Stephen Few, 'Information Dashboard Design'): dials/gauges 'say little and do so poorly' — they waste space, use decoration (bezels, needles, tick faces) that carries no data, and 'have you knee-jerk reacting to routine variation... and fail to tell you when there is a true signal of change toward or away from your target.' Few's prescribed replacement for a single measure-vs-target-vs-qualitative-range is the bullet graph: one bar (the measure), a tick/marker (the target), and background bands of one hue at varying intensity (the qualitative ranges: poor/satisfactory/good) — all on a linear baseline. (staceybarr.com, perceptualedge.com, tableau.com/blog/bullet-graphs-beat-gauge-charts)
- Perceptual-accuracy research (Cleveland & McGill) underlies the critique: humans judge position-along-a-common-scale and length far more accurately than angle. A radial gauge encodes the value as an angle/arc read against a curved baseline, which is measurably harder to compare precisely than a bar/number on a straight line — this is why gauges are worse for COMPARISON (this case vs that case, this week vs last week) even though they can still be fine for a single glanceable value with no comparison task.
- NN-Group-style KPI-card guidance converges on: big number first ('$1,248,000' parses faster than '104% of target'), a label, a directional delta vs the prior period, and an inline sparkline — 'a KPI card with a sparkline gives you both the current number and the trend in ~60px of vertical space; a KPI card without one is just a number.' The F-shaped scan pattern implies the single most important metric belongs top-left of a header/dashboard.
- Real SOC/security products that DO use a radial/ring form for a single headline score all restrict it to ONE hero metric, not a repeated widget: Microsoft Secure Score shows a percentage + a 'Secure Score zone' (good/okay/bad, org-defined) plus trend/points-regressed views — the ring is one summary artifact accompanied by explicit trend and contribution breakdowns, not the whole story. Elastic Security's entity risk score is presented as a 0-100 number with an expandable 'risk contributions' table (top-10 alerts + their contribution score + asset-criticality weighting) — the number is the glanceable anchor, the breakdown is the explanation. (learn.microsoft.com/defender-xdr/microsoft-secure-score, elastic.co/docs .../entity-risk-scoring, .../view-analyze-risk-score-data)
- Security-rating vendors (BitSight, SecurityScorecard) that need cross-entity COMPARISON (many companies/vendors at once) deliberately avoid gauges: BitSight uses a 250-900 numeric scale with A-F letter grades per risk vector; SecurityScorecard uses A-F letters as the primary 'at a glance' encoding. Letters/numbers on a shared scale, not one dial per company — because dials don't tile or compare well across a list.
- Radial/donut progress-ring literature (Domo, chartengine, shadcn/Recharts pattern library): a 'gauge chart' = needle or fill against a fixed scale; a 'progress ring' = filled arc showing percent-complete, no needle. Modern implementations (shadcn's 'radial chart with custom shape,' built on Recharts, using `endAngle` for a partial arc + a centered big-number label) favor the progress-ring/arc-fill style over needles — needles read as more decorative and are harder to read precisely than a filled arc terminating at a value.
- Glanceable/ambient-display research (CHI/DIS literature on 'glanceable peripheral displays') establishes a distinct design goal from analytical dashboards: a display meant to be read in under a second, with minimal cognitive load, to answer ONE question ('is this okay right now?'). This is the legitimate use case for a radial gauge form — it is NOT meant to support precise reading or side-by-side comparison, only fast triage of a single status.
- AI-confidence/trust-calibration UX research is explicit that an AI's certainty must be visually DISTINCT from a factual/derived metric, using badges/pills/qualitative labels ('High/Medium/Low confidence') rather than a second precise-looking number or gauge, because a false sense of precision on a probabilistic value erodes calibrated trust; visualizing uncertainty (bands/ranges, not point values) measurably improves trust especially among skeptical users (frontiersin.org 10.3389/fcomp.2025.1464348, aiuxplayground.com/pattern/confidence-score).
- Our current `webui/src/soc/components/RiskGauge.tsx` already implements several of the 'right' choices per this research: a half-circle (not full-circle, avoiding the 12-o'clock-ambiguity problem), arc-FILL not a needle, `currentColor`-driven severity banding on the SAME element (fixing an earlier near-white-sliver bug), a big centered number (`{score}/100`) as the primary read, AND a redundant non-color text band label ('Critical/High/Medium/Low') plus a full-sentence `<title>`/`aria-labelledby` for a11y — i.e., it already follows the 'never encode meaning in color/angle alone' rule. What it is missing, per the research above, is: a trend/delta indicator, a visible threshold marker tied to the auto-close/escalate policy, and a documented list of what feeds the score (an Elastic-style contribution breakdown).

**Concrete recommendations for us:**
- Keep exactly ONE gauge as the 'hero' widget per screen (Overview page global Active-Risk-Index; CaseDetail's single case risk). Never render more than one RiskGauge in the same view, and never render it inside a list/table row — for the Cases list and any N-item context, keep using the existing flat severity/risk/impact/priority CHIPS (already in the design system per CLAUDE.md), not mini-gauges: per Cleveland-McGill, angle/arc reading doesn't support the row-to-row comparison a table needs, and N gauges don't tile.
- Add a delta/trend affordance next to (not inside) the gauge: a small `+Δ` / `-Δ` with an up/down triangle and 'vs 24h ago' (or vs the TimeRangePicker window already used elsewhere in Round 6), matching the KPI-card pattern (`StatCard`/`KpiTile` already have delta-arrow logic — reuse that component/util beside RiskGauge rather than inventing a second delta implementation). This is the single highest-value addition: a gauge shows *level*, never *trend*, and trend is what an analyst scanning a header actually needs first.
- Render a threshold notch on the gauge's track at the operator-configured AutoClosePolicy escalation boundary (e.g., a 2px tick/marker at the score where TRUE_POSITIVE-class auto-escalate would trigger, drawn on the muted track path in the same `<svg>`, using the same deterministic band boundaries — 74/48/22 — already defined in `palette.ts`). This turns the gauge from a static illustration into the bullet-graph idea (measure + qualitative bands + a target marker) without a rewrite, and it answers the analyst's real question ('how close is this to auto-escalating') that a plain dial cannot.
- Add a small info affordance (reuse the existing `HelpTip` component) next to the gauge that spells out the band boundaries in words/numbers ('Critical ≥ 74 · High ≥ 48 · Medium ≥ 22 · Low < 22') — never make the viewer reverse-engineer thresholds from arc position alone; this is the textual redundancy bullet-graphs get for free from their labeled bands.
- Do NOT reuse RiskGauge (or any second gauge) for the LLM's verdict confidence. Keep 'Risk Index' (deterministic, composite of severity+correlation+enrichment — SIEM/engine-asserted) and 'AI Verdict Confidence' (the investigator's stated certainty) as two visually distinct, non-interchangeable elements: Risk Index = the existing arc gauge; AI Confidence = a small qualitative badge/pill ('High confidence · 82%' as secondary sub-text, not the headline) placed inside the AI-assessment section of the case-story view, never inside the SIEM-facts section. This directly serves the ask to separate SIEM-asserted facts from AI-derived assessment, and avoids the well-documented trust-calibration failure of making a probabilistic estimate look as precise/authoritative as a measured fact.
- For the case 'story' view specifically: put the Risk Index gauge in the FACTS-derived summary strip (it's computed deterministically from signals/correlation/enrichment — cite this in a tooltip: 'derived from severity, correlation, enrichment — not the LLM'), and keep the AI narrative/verdict/confidence entirely separate, visually (different card, different iconography, maybe the existing 'Why' tab) so a reviewer never mistakes an LLM-derived confidence number for a hard measured score.
- In case-list / dense contexts and anywhere risk needs to be scanned across many rows or compared over time (Standup, Metrics, MITRE coverage trend), use a linear bullet-style encoding instead of any gauge: a slim horizontal bar with background severity bands + a single filled segment + the number as text — this is a ~1-day build reusing the existing `charts.tsx` primitives and is strictly more comparable across rows than repeated dials.
- Guard against the historical 'Active Risk Index glitch' class of bug (referenced in the CLAUDE.md history) by adding/keeping a regression test that the arc's `stroke-dashoffset` transitions FROM the previous rendered value, not from 0/stale on every poll refresh — i.e., don't key/remount `<RiskGauge>` on each fetch; only update the `score` prop so the existing 500ms CSS transition animates smoothly rather than resetting. Add a Vitest case asserting dashoffset math for score=0 and score=100 edge cases explicitly (both are already handled correctly in the current path-length formula, but pin them with a test).
- Keep the current semicircle + arc-fill + `currentColor`-banding + big-number-with-text-label pattern as-is for the single-gauge use case — it already matches the best current practice observed across Microsoft Secure Score / Elastic risk score / shadcn-Recharts radial patterns (partial arc, no needle, centered numeral+caption). Do not 'upgrade' it to a full donut or add tick-mark decoration around the rim — that would reintroduce the decoration Few criticizes without adding legibility.
- Where the product needs an executive/aggregate view of MANY entities' risk (fleet-wide posture, e.g., an org-level rollup across all sources), do not tile gauges — use the BitSight/SecurityScorecard pattern: a sortable table/heatmap of numeric score + qualitative band chip per entity, so multiple risk values are comparable on a shared scale.

**Pitfalls to avoid:**
- Multiple gauges in one view (e.g., one dial per case in a list, or 4 KPI dials across the top of Overview) — defeats the one legitimate reason to use a gauge (a single glanceable hero value) and makes cross-item comparison strictly worse than bars/numbers because angle/arc reading is less precise than position-on-a-shared-baseline (Cleveland-McGill).
- Needle-style gauges, or gauges with printed tick marks/labels around the rim — pure decoration that adds visual clutter without adding legible information (Few's core critique); arc-fill (progress-ring) reads faster and is what modern SOC/security products (Elastic, Microsoft) and modern chart libraries (shadcn/Recharts radial pattern) actually ship.
- Encoding the value in color/angle alone with no numeral or text label — fails WCAG non-text-content / color-alone criteria and fails colorblind users; our current component already avoids this (number + text band + full-sentence title) — never regress that when 'restyling.'
- A gauge with no trend/delta — a dial only ever shows current level, never whether things are getting better or worse; per KPI-card research this makes it strictly less useful than a big-number+sparkline+delta combo for header/glance contexts, so always pair it with an explicit delta rather than relying on the dial alone.
- Reusing one 'gauge' component for both a deterministic composite risk score and a probabilistic LLM confidence value — conflates 'fact-derived measurement' with 'AI-stated certainty' visually, undermining the explicit ask to separate SIEM-asserted facts from AI-derived assessment, and is a documented trust-miscalibration anti-pattern in AI-UX literature (a probabilistic estimate should never LOOK as precise/authoritative as a measured metric).
- Remounting/re-keying the gauge component on every data refresh so the fill animation restarts from zero each poll — reads as flickering/broken (this class of bug was flagged in this project's own history as the 'Active Risk Index glitch'); always animate value-to-value, not zero-to-value, on refresh.
- Adding a gauge merely because it 'looks like a dashboard' — if the surrounding UI doesn't need a single ambient/glanceable status (e.g., an analytical page where the user is actively comparing risk across time or entities), a bullet-style bar or big-number+trend will out-perform a dial; reserve the dial for the one true 'is this okay right now' header moment (Overview hero metric, CaseDetail hero metric).

**Sources:**
- https://www.staceybarr.com/measure-up/why-dashboard-dials-and-gauges-are-useless-for-kpis/
- https://www.perceptualedge.com/blog/?p=1423
